Iminute, hut the Ma.von held that that course was co cruet 1 unless the Council desired to confirm the minute. The TOWN CLERK ssid the withdrawal of resolntions in that way had been the practice of the Council for the last twenty years. It had been done hundreds of times ' to his retollection by members in that. chamber. If the Council agreed to what was proposed there was an end of it, but if the Council did not approve an amend- ment could be proposed that the whole of the minutes - should be considered and approved. The minutes were contirmed with the exceptioa of the ' resolution withdrawn by Alderman Francomb. The following is a copy of the letter which had been ' addressed to each member of the Council by the Non- Trading Association :—" Dear sir,—Lawson's Contract.— We think we need only call your attention to the extra- ordinary resolution of the Water Cmtmittee which appears in the Council Agenda for next Tuesday's meet- ing, to insure your unhesitating aud indignant decision to vote against its confirmation. The resolution, which has been put forward at the instigation of the Town Clerk, rends as follows :—(That the Committee consent to the appointment of Mr. George Hill of Manchester, as arbitrator to settle the dispute between Mr. George , Lawson and the Corporation and that an application be !) made to the County Court Judge for this appointment.) , In view of the fact that the Council, a short time ago speciallyappointed Mr. Garside, C.E. (who is ma expert on the precise kind of work done by Mr. Lawson), to thoroughly investigate the enormons claims for extras put forward by the contractor, it would not only be the height of folly, but an injustice and an insult to the ratepayers, to make an appointment, or take any steps to settle with Mr. Lawson, until Mr. Garside's ,report has been received, and we feel sure you will take care to be present at Tuesday's Council meeting to , vote against the confirmation of the above resolution, the object of which must be very clear to yon.—l am, your ob,,dient servant, for the Warrington Non-trading Association.—JAMES DODDS, Chairman?' DEALING WITH TRAMWAY EMPLOYES. The Electricity and Tramways Committee recom- mended that all reports against men employed by the Tramways Department be made direct to the Manager, that he be folly authorised to use his discretion in dealing with the cases, and that only such'. CR9O9 as he thinks advisable he brought before the Cormittee. Alderman SMETIICRST, in moving the confirmation of the minutes, explained that the Committee were making an application to borrow £lO,OOO to cover the cost of extensions during the next three years. The extensions were mainly in connection with electric cables which it was intended they should carry. along Wilderspool-road and Chester-road. Coencillor STOTT moved an amendment that the minute dealing with tramway employees be not con- firmed. He thought the men in all departments should be given facilities to place any grievance before-the responsible committee. In other Corporations where the power of dismissal, &a., had been put in the hands of individuals the men had suffered. In the discussion which ensued it was pointed out that the Tramways Manager had already the power of dealing with-the men and that the men had the right nnder Standing Orders of the3Council to appeal to the Comolittee.

cDouGALL's pewit SELF-RAISING FLOUR D. The Examiner, Saturday, January 13, 1906.

(ALL lizollTA RESERVED.]

BY

HOW TO PLAY 13RIDGE,

"CUT CAVENDISH (rfutior of " .Ilow to TVin at Bridge," se.).

Bridge ! What a fascination surrounds the very mune of the popular idol. Whist, chess, billiards, all bow the knee before the newcomer's victorious career.

Indeed not to play bridge will soon be re- garded as an offence in society, and will one of these days doubtless be punishable by a mild term of imprisonment.. Already a heavy fine is inflicted on those misguided mortals who play the game with little thought and less study. Their fine has generally to be met at the close of the rubber, when the psychological moment has arrived to pity, pay, pay. Nor is there any excuse for . the ignorance which is se frequently displayed at at Bridge table. The game is not nearly so scientific or intricate as whist, bound up as the latter is in innumerable leads and conventions. Consequent- ly the Bridge player has only to master a few of its leading principles to find himself a consider- able way on the road to sucdess. A short review of these principles, therefore, may not be without interest and instruction to that large body of Bridge players which has hitherto been satisfied to muddle along • rather than study the book and the general public cannot be altogether blamed, as the majority of books on bridge extend over several hundreds of pages, and often leave the student in grave doubt as to whether he stands on his head or his heels.

(Laughter.) Other Councillors considered that incandescent lights were much more preferable for street lighting purposes. Eventuany the minute was withdrawn. STREET 'PAV/iciG.

PENKETH.

OLD FoLKs' TREAT.—This ever-popular function was billed for Friday night last, and the energy and enthusiasm shown in the working of it showed that it is in a llourishine state. This treat is the 18th anni- versary, and though the original promoters are not now connected with the working of it, they have a worthy lot of successors who make every endeavour to make each treat better than the preceding one. The treat is supported by public subscription, and the aid asked for is willingly given, and indeed it would be difficult to find a more praiseworthy object, for the old folks would miss their annual gathering terribly. The treat was held in the Wesleyan schoolroom and the guests from Sankey were conveyed there in a covered 'bus which took them back at the conclusion. The ladies' com- mittee had been busily engaged in their labour of getting a substantial ten. and at 6.30 the party number- ing 101 sat down and did full justice to the tempting Iviands. While the room was being arranged for tle I entertainment a fine gramaphone, the property of Mr. Clay, was in use, and the various selections were much enjoyed. The President, Mr. J. C. Thompson, presided over the entertainment and this year the evening was taken over by the 'Leyland Troupe of pierotts. They gave a very entertaining programme, and the humorous efforts of Messrs. McKay, Madden and Taylor were very mirth-provoking. The only local contriteition was given by the Misses and Master Wolstencroft, who, in fancy dress, danced two pretty dances in capti- vating style and were recalled deservedly for their efforts. Durine an interval Mr. Thompson addressed the meeting, and expressed his pleasure at the good7y muster, and trusted that they wotild meet again many more times. He feelingly referred to the loss of some who had for years been regular attendants at the annual treatand read a letter from the late Vicar, Rev. E. S. Jackson, who had sent his best wishes to all his old parishioners. Mr. Thompson then proposed a vote of thanks to the ladies who had worked so hard to get the tea ready, and those who had helped to entertain them during the evening. Mr. Clay seconded and the motion was carried with great unanimity. Dr. Murray voiced the feeling of the gathering in response; and Mr. John Atherton, a guest, supported his remarks. Thanks are also due, to Messrs, T. and J. Drinabin,who had willingly come forward and decorated the room very tastefully for the occasion. After the concert slipper was pro- vided, and parcels containing tea, coffee, and sugar, given to each woman while each man received an ounce of tobacco and a pipe. Those unable to attend were not forgotten. and their parcels were forwarded sub- sequently. Many expressions of satisfaction came trom the old folks, at their reception, and all voted the treat a great success. The committee responsible are Messrs. .1. Thompson president, J. T. Ambler, chair- man, Messrs. W. Taylor. M. H. Ireland, H. J. Mainlev H. Fairclon Rontledge hon. treasurer, and R. NV Houghton hon. secretary.

STOCKTON HEATH.

FIRST PRINCIPLES OF THE GAME.

Probably everybody interested in Bridge knows that in the play of the hand the game closely resembles Dummy Whist. No sooner has the lead been made than Dummy's cards come down, and the dealer plays both his partner's and his own bands. Previously the dealer has had the privilege of declaring the trump suit or of making No Trumps. And in this connection it may. be remarked that the tricks and honours differ in value according to the suit declared trumps. Thus every trick beyond six, in spades counts 2, in clubs 4, in diamonds 6, in hearts 8, and in no trumps 12. After the declaration the player on the dealer's left has the option of doubling the scoring value of every trick. If he does not care to double, he says to his partner, " May I play t" Yes, please," or " I double," are the recognised forms of reply. Re-doubling may follow, but when all such doubling is at an end, the play begins. .. First and foremost comes the " declaration," not the least important element in the fortunes of the game. And whilst on this subject let me emphasise one point which is worthy of more attention than it generally obtains. Be careful to take into account ,the capabilities of the player who has the management of the two hands. If he is a round player, the declaration may be made on the outside value of the cards ; otherwise a light declaration is likely to prove an expensive luxury. Before deciding always consult the score, and if you can see your way out on a black snit by all means make that suit trumps. After all, the main object is to win the game. However, with the score love all, your, hopes are centred in No Trumps, hearts Or diamonds. And whilst re- membering that to call on a light red suit is a dangerous practice to adopt, a thin No Tramper is a factor in the game never to be despised. Providing therefore, that you hold a hand some- what above average strength, and that you can get in on three suits, let it be No trumps, un- less you are the happy possessor of five or more hearts, when hearts will doubtless constitute the soundest declaration. Two or three aces are not indispensable to a successful No Tramper. I do not wish to dispute that aces are useful cards, but they are far Irons being everything.

BED Olt BLACK SCIT DECLABATIONS.

In the early stages of the gtillTh hearts ought only to be called by the dealer when he can see his way to five or more tricks. If the dealer is unlikely to get more than four tricks, and vet makes .hearts trumps, he is actually expecting his partner's hand to contain only one trick less than his own. This is an absurdity on the face of it, seeing that the dealer cm claim the , in- estimable advantage of having made his own trumps. Remember that when you make a red- suit declaration, you are the attacking party, and the odds should assuredly favour your -thance of success. To make an original diamond declaration you require considerable strength in that suit. You will seldom win the game from love, but you can hope to get to 18, a very useful start. Of course with four honours in hearts or diamonds you are well advised to 'benefit by such, good fortune, even though your hand is painfully weak outside trumps. 46 or 84 above the line is a little item not to be too lightly disregarded. But unless you hold a superfluity of honours it is dangerous to make a red suit or a club declare on less than five of a suit. On the other hand, six hearts or six diamonds, however small your cards may be, should see a red' suit declared ; but with five of a suit to an honour never risk a red declaration unless some strength is shown in other directions.

One other point in relation to a red snit de- clare is wcrthy the attention of the dealer. I allude to the influence his band is likely to have on his partner's. For example, whenever it ap- pears highly improbable to the dealer that dummy can make a red suit—perhaps the dealer bolds eight of the red cards—then he must be prepared to accept a mild risk. Let him de- clare hearts or diamonds rather than a pass, providing the while that his hand is a bit above the average. If a risk must be taken at all, now is the time to take it.

===== 0002601_19060113_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
AFTER. THE DECLARATION.

These points having been mastered, it only remains for me to reiterate that when you . are dealer you are attacking. Consequently no trumps, hearts and diamonds are alone open to you to declare on, unless the score is sufficiently advanced to admit of your reaching 30 with a club or spade call. Now and then, however, the dealer's band will contain nothing approachinr, a trick, when it will be his business to make; spades. Cut your losses when there seems small likelihood of success crowning your efforts. how, for in- stance, is your partner to know that there .is trick in your hand 'e Yet if you leave the de- claration to him you expect him to get seven tricks " on his own." In the natural order of events no trumps, or a red suit, will be the an- nouncement, and the dealer will go down badly. Let this happen more than once, and Dummy will soon tight shy of calling up to the full strength of his hand, a very serious lirtinlicap. When the declaration is passed, dummy must be guided by very much the seine principles as the dealer. He must, however, take fewer risks, as the fact of his cards being exposed weakens their trick-making value. When, therefore, he is not fortunate enough to possess an attacking hand, spades is the declaration, a simple rule to follow and yet a golden one. Even if the de- clarer has no spade he must not be deterred from his purpose. A weak heart, diamond or club hand- is often made to look very foolish when doubled.

===== 0002601_19060113_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
TTIE DOTAILE.

On doubling, my advice shall be direct and to the point. As a rule, double only when you call see the odd trick in yonr own hand. Against spades you eau take some legitimate risks, more especially should you hold trump strengtii. To double to the score is not always to be encour- aged, as it shows the dealer where the strewgth, out against him lies. At any rate, in almost; every instance ,the, dohbler should be sitting over the declarer, or he is likely to be hoist with his own petard. Redoubling unless the score de- mands it, should only be attempted in the event of a certainty. For you may depend upon it your opponents hare not doubled without sub- stantial reasons of their own.

• Coming, to the play GI the lintal, considerable ituport.miee attliehes to the lead. in No Trumps lhe blind lead, as it h.. called. should hail from your longest, slut, which 'our partner re- haus tis early in the game as lie can.

In a smt declaration you must be guided by your own common sense. Oftener thun Hut re- tain the lend when you ere eble to. Dummy's hirnd is then exposed, and you must be guided during the rest of the hand by iui earas. Dummy you lead through his strong plain suits, whilst when Dummy is on your right you lead up to his weak plain'snits. II i is en admirable plan of cempaign to adopt. NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Rugby Football.

[By " Ovalist."]

Either Warrington's form was a fissh in the pan on Saturday, or the men have suddenly decided that it was time they set about improving the position of the Club. In entertaining Leeds they were meeting some of the strongest opposition in the League, for the visitors have been strong challengers for the Leage championship all the season. Moreover they came here with a powerful side, whereas the home rear division was practically decimated by the absence of such men as Fish, Isherwood, Hockenhall and Brooks. The Warrington men, however, rose to the occasion in the fashion which they used to be famous for and completely put the Leeds men out of court by their dashing methods. Finally a very gratifying and welcome:win by eight points to nil was recorded.

Saturday was about the worst day for football which has been experienced this season. Rain fell heavily and persistently and the ground was in a sodden condition. This was held to improve the home team's chances, since it discounted the finer points of the game, and neutralised the Leeds team's advantage in the re ir. Probably on a dry day they would have given a passing exhibition too smart for the reserve backs on the home side, but under the difficult conditions on Saturday they were beaten at every point. 11'hough the ground was so soft that football appeared almost impossible, yet few more interesting and exciting games have been seeu at Wilderspool this season. The forward play of the homesters was positively brilliaet and the sweeping rushes of the front-rankers were well backed up by speedy men in the rear. Leeds played a hard game bat they were thrown quite off their football balance, and never looked like taking any points back with them.

In the first half the visitors had the advantage of a strong wind and were generally expected to score before the interval. 'Warrington buckled to with energy and we saw for once what the heavy forwards could do when they liked. The Leeds men are a bulky lot, but they were hustled off the ball time and again, and only for about ten minutes were they really dangerous. Warrington maintained a clean sheet up to the change of ends, and were then prime favourities. They quickly broke up to the Leeds defence on resuming and Kenyon dribbled over at top speed for Preston to add a goaL Hostilities were maintained, and Hughes failing to deal with an awkward handful, Preston, Shut:cars, and Poardman, the home heavyweights burst through at the corner, the first named scoring a try. This raised the total to eight points,and though Leeds threatened once with a really good passing run, nothing startling happened arid Warrington won as stated.

Though we know that Leeds hare a clever back division, a spectator would be bound to conclude on Saturday. that Warrington were superior in this department. It was no day for nassing,aad the Leeds backs seemed unable to adapt themselves to ftvitwork like the home men. Lloyd, at half back, played his best game, and is to be complimented on his plucky and heady display. He frequently just tipped-the ball beyond the Leeds centres, and then Kenyon's fine turn of speed came in useful. Both the latter and Jones did well while little fault could be found with the wings or the forwards. Thomas was übiquitous. The style of play just suited him, and on Saturday's form he is without an equal in the Northern Union as a I loose forward. He practically demoralised Ward and I Mosely at times. Knight was hardly so safe as could be wished, but luck favoured him. On the Leeds side " stars " like Llewellyn and Thomas were inconspicu- ous, Naylor (full bark') and Jenkins (left wing) being the most serviceable. Jim Hughes, late of Widnes made a fair first appearance for Leeds. As a rule, however, he does nothing against Warringten and Saturday tvas no exception.

Instead of being at the top, as they were a month' ago, Leeds are now seventh, but some of the clubs above, such as Leigh and Huddersfield, have not bad. quite the same arduous campaign. Runcorn is another team which keeps a fair average at the expense of some of the League sm4ll fry. Oldham have a fine record, but they are closely pressed by Ffunsiet witi five matches .in hand, have an almost equal percentage.

THE DEALER'S GAME.

The dealer whenever he has made an attack- ing suit declaration should draw trumps on every available occasion until he has exhausted what trumps there may be out against him. I'o nurse trumps nearly always leads to fatal re- sults, as it often enables the opposing party to advantageously dispose of several of their little ones. Again, with trumps out of the way the dealer has every opportunity afforded him of establishing his long. suit. Above all things before miming any risk be as- sured that you cannot lose the game lIS a conse- quence. Do not be tempted to finesse if the genie can be won by playing a winning card. rear in mind to consult the score at all stages of the game, mid in relation to this, make a special mental note of. the importance of 6 and 24_as stages to success.

Finesses are often useful, although you ought very rarely to finesse to your partner's init:al lead. Yon may finesse in trumps oftener than in a plain suit, and when you are dealer, end can finesse from either baud, finesse up to the hand from which you would rather the lead sprang.

Regarding the discard, against a No Trumper your first discard should, whenever practicable, be from your weakest suit. Against an attacking suit declaration it should come from your strongest suit. This sort of information it„ in- valuable to your partner, who in every. instance draws his deductions from your -first discard. At Bridge the opportunity sometimes occurs of holding up the command of a suit. No* if by so doing you believe that you can defeat your opponent's ends, on no account part with your leading -card until the dealer is no longer able to put Dummy in. Unblocking. on the other hand, is equally important. When you think you are likely to get into your partner's way, the simplest method to adopt is to keep the smallest card of your suit to the last. Of course, I have only, as it were, touched on the fringe of the game, but if the above princi- ples are acquired, they will be found to beer the test .of serious Bridge. More games will be won by the reader and fewer games lost.

NON-TRADERS AND THE ELECTION.

CANDIDATES' REPLIES TO QUESTIONS. Both in Favour of an Amendment of the Law.

777-'7:777. - v . , A

The Warrington Non-Trading Association, through their chairman, Mr. James Dodds, submitted ques- tions to each candidate with regard to the trading question as understdod in Warrington. We append a copy of the questions, and the respective answers of Mr. A. H. Crosfield and Mr. R. Pierpoint. Warrington Non-trading Association. !.ht Penketh House, neer Warrington, 29th December, 1905 Sir,—T am desired, as Chairman of the Non-trading Association, to respectfully ask you, as a candidate for the representation of Warrington at the forthcoming Parliamentary Election, the following questions, namely :- 1. Are you of opinion that no representative elected to any public body should derive pecuniary profit either directly or indirectly, by reason of his mem- bership of such public body ; and if so- 2. Would you vote for a bill in parliament which would make it illegal for members of County, Borough and District Councils to trade with the Council of which they are rnembers,whether through limited Companies or otherwise? Awaiting the favour of en early reply, I am, Sir, Your obedient Servant, JAMES DODDS. Chairmsn.

St. Austin's,

Warrington. Dec. 30th, 1905. Dear Sir,—l am obliged by your letter of yesterday. in reply to the questious of the Warrington Non- Trading Association, I have to say that in my opinion it would be for the public good that no member of a County, Borough or District Council should directly or indirectly, through a Limited Company or otherwise, derive any pecuniary profit from business transactions with the Council to which it belongs, unless—in the case of a Company—the shares in which he is interested form such a small proportion of the Company's capital as to be practically immaterial. I am therefore quite willing to vote for a Bill making such trading illegal. I think it necessary to make the above proviso, lest a firm, in which a member of a Council has only an insignificant interest should be debarred from contracting with the local public body, or an eligible and useful man kept out of the said public body by a cause all but technical.

Tam, dear Sir, Yours faithfully, (Signed) ROBERT PI ERPOINT. To James Dodds, Esq.

Patten Arms Hotel, Warrington, January 6th, 1906. James Dodds Esq. Penketh, Warrington. Dear Sir,—Replying to your favour of the 29th : With respect to the Non-Trading question, I think my friends on both sides have in the main understood my opinions from the commencement of this serious local controversy. I bold that no man in the Council should make im- proper use of his position there to the disadvantage to the town and to his own advantage. Almost the whole ed my time and energies haying been occupied of recent years with my private business, and with the questions of Imperial politics it has been utterly impossible ter me to follow in all their detail thecharges made against individuals on the score of their trading with the Cor- poration,and therefore I do not make personal charges against anyone. With these preliminaries may I say I have never approved of the principle of unlimited trading. I have been opposed to it if only because; I think it ; much too dangerous a precedent. How and to what ; extent trading through Limited Companies should be ; limited by law is a complicated question, and I should be willing and anxious to consider carefully any measure brought forward in Parliament to solve this problem. OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Wycliffe Church. Distribution of Bibles.

A special New Year's service was held on Sunday morning at Wycliffe Church, which was conducted by, the Rev. Alfred A. Lee, assistant minister of the Emmanuel Congregational Church, Bootle. In the morning, after the singing of a dedication hymn com- posed by Mr. A. Corney, the Rev. J. Yonge presented 24 Bibles to the members of his annual class. In doing so he stated that the class had existed for 25 years, and during its history upwards of 700 Bibles had been pre- sented. Many of this large number of scholars are now scattered far and wide over the United Kingdom and over the whole world. But the Wycliffe Bible formed for them a link of connection with the Church of their youth, which many of them cherished with loyal and grateful affection. At the evening service, the Pastor preached upon the Wycliffe motto for 190 S, " Rejoicing in hope." At each service there was a large congregation. Wyglie P.S.A. Society. A very good meeting of this Society was- held on - Sunday. Mr. G. Wallington presided, and wished all present a very prosperous and happy New Year. The Iles,. John Yonge (the President) gave the address, and took for his subjebt " Quit you like men." As the three Hebrew youths were fearless of the flames, the fear of God was uppermost in their? minds; they honoured God and God delivered them. The young man of to-day gloried in his strength, but that counted for little with- out strength from God to body, mind, and heart. He wished, he said, these words to be a motto for them for the present year, then it would give a greater prosperity to the P.S.A. He instanced the great blessing the move- ment had been to many homes, and to the town, and hoped it would be of still greater influence to a larger circle. Mr. R. H. Pearson rendered two violin solos in his usual pleasing manner. Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. Bac., presided at the organ. Mr. C. Cotton and the Secretary took part in the rneeting.—The Secretary gave a hearty welcome to two new members. Total now, 537.

THE SPIDER TO THE FLY.

If you are unable to obtain our Toilet Soaps. either from Tour Chemist or Grocer, please write direct to the Company, -46, 38, & 40, Broad Street, House, New Broad Street.l,ondon, enclosinz Stamps for as mans, Tablets as are required, when they will be forwarded ;mg free. Agents Wanted.

BY .T. DOUGALL REID,

Author of "In the Grenadier Company," "The Bullers o' Buchan," "Mabel Vynees Lover," "The Cab with the Black Horse," &c.

An esteemed citizen is, generally speaking,.a man who hasn't been found out. If Truth— stark Truth—were but to come up from the bottom of her well and take the bandage from the eyes of her sister Justice, there would be no esteemed 'citizens—or mighty few. The foregoing, and a deal more of the same sort, would very well have expressed the mind or Colin Bain when he discovered that pretty, b it, truth to tell, railer flighty, Jessie French, was disposed to throw him over for a richer, and, as she hold, more worthy, admirer. Colin had admitted that the admirer in question might b richer' although he had his doubts, but d intirred flatly to the "more worthy" part of t m verdict, whereupon Jessie had overwhelmed lfm with the information that his, as yet, woknown rival was a gentleman, with heaps of money, who had had a real gold chronometer and a bert presented to him in a public hall as an etteemed citizen, while he—Col—was only a common compositor, with thirty-five shillings a week, and an old mother to keep. Then, htving crushed the daring Colin, she had gone of with her empty head in the air, leaving him t) think the matter over during his solitary homeward walk across Glasgow Green. He did think it over, and the longer lie thought the less he was pleased. Jessie, with her dainty fimre, yellow hair, and sea-blue eyes, was a haittedly the very prettiest of all the -girls employed in Strang's mill, a factory noted for Ii wing more bonnie lasses in it than any other in Glasgow; but that did not constitute a quite s.tisfactory reason for her having a gentleman hver, Colin thought—that was, if the man meant well by the girl, for in that ease the least could end in for her .would be being jilted. Men of position and education Want more in the women they marry than mere beauty, and dearly though he loved her, Colin saw clearly that beauty was about all Jessie had as qualify- ing her for possible entrance into "class " society. Many of her companion-workers were girls of wide reading and more or less refined tistes, hut with this group she had never become friendly. Dancing, flirting, and generally making the opposite sex feel sorry for itself, embodied her idea of well-spent time, and as her mother—her father was dead—bad long ceased to exercise any control over her, she did pretty much as she pleased. The result was that although she was at heart as good .a girl as ever walked, her mode of life kept that goodness a strictly stationary quantity, admitting of neither increase nor diminution—something to be thank- ful for, possibly, that last. "Ah, well," said Colin, half aloud, as he stopped and looked up at the rain-swept April sky, in which the stars were flashing faintly, "if she's in earnest about that—that man, and he about her, I won't stand in their way. • She has a right to please herself, I suppose, but —but she might have told me sooner.' The conflict between love and anger audible in his voice did not last long. As usual with him, love got the upper hand, turned anger out of doors, dragged hope in by the ears, and set him spinning excuses for Jessie with an earnestness that spoke volumes for his ingenuity.

Quitting the Green, he was making his way towards his home in Great Hamilton-street, when he was arrested by a feminine voice. "Ye must be in a hurry, Colin, when ye canna see your frien's." Turning, he beheld at his side a tell, hand- some girl, in whose smartly-dressed figure and smiling face 1-1, recognised Jeanie Teller' the sweetheart of his own particular chum, Alec Glen.

"No, in no special hurry, Jennie," he replied, with a faint smile; "but I was thinking, and didn't see you." "Like enough. But your thoughts must have been black, to give ye a face like that—sour as vinegar. Have you an' Jessie had a quarrel to-night?" They were standing under a lamp, the light of which enabled the questioner to note the flush that rose on her hearer's dark face.

"How did you know I was with her?" he asked, evasively. "That wafina hard to guess," said she, with a laugh. - "You're dressed to kill—got them all on. Jessie stays over in Govan-street, an' you're only getting home to Great Hamilton; street at ten o'clock. Oh, it's too hard; ask me something easier." "Well, I was across the Green, seeing her home," admitted Colin, the flush on his face deepening under her raillery. "An' you quarrelled?" "In a sort of way—yes. But we'll be able to make it up again, I expect." "You don't expect anything of the kind, if you know anything of what's going on," said Jeanie, with a change of look and tone so sudden that Colin was startled. "You may intend to make it up, but I don't think she does. If she did, she wouldn't do as she's doing; and if you arc a man, you'll either make her walk straight or walk by herself, so far as you are concerned. You know about this swell mash of hers ?"

Colin nodded; speech was difficult at the moment. •

"I.7mph ! Well, I saw her with him last night, an' so did Aloe. We were in the Queen's Park, an' came on them by chance. We saw thorn both plainly enough—an', Colin, ye can take this from Alec as well as from ine, that while yon man may mean the 6traight thing. the chances are a hundred against. He's a deal older than she is he is not at all like a fit match for her, an' he has a bad face. T always knew that Jessie was thoughtless, but I did think she had enough sense to keep her from running such risks as she's doing. If she doesn't watch herself, she'll end where poor Mirren Slade did three years ago—in the Clyde." In her indignation, the speaker had to a large extent forgotten the feelings of her hearer—till she was reminded of them by a hoarse cry, and, looking closer, saw his white face set in horror unspeakable. "I didn't mean to hurt you, Cohn," she said, mith quick contrition. "But there are some things that must be said, and if ye werena Alec' friend, I wouldn't speak at all." Ita all right, Jeanie," replied Colin, bravely. "It isn't nice to hear, of course, but---"

"But you haven't heard the worst," put ic Jeanie, quickly. "You haven't heard what it was that made us make up our minds to risk speaking —and speaking out. Alec was to see you the morn, but when I met you I thought I wouldn't wait." "And what is the worst, then ?" asked he, the tremor in his voice belying the smile on his face. "Speak out, Jeanie; twenty-six is not so easily scared as six, you know." "Well, this is Tuesday," replied Jeanie, slowly, "an'—an' " she's going to Edinburgh with that man on Friday. If she had expected either answer or demon- stration, she was disappointed. Colin stood still and silent, and after glancing at him uneasily once or twice, she went on : "I heard her telling some of the girls in the wcrk this morning—just as if it had been any- thing to boast about! She said that Mr. Aylmer was going to Edinburgh on business, an was going to take her with him for the day—that if she couldn't get leave from her work, she'd take it. That's all, Colin ; an' now, if you'll let me say it, you should make an end of this, for your own sake, if not for hers. Find out what he is, and make her choose between you. If I were a raa,n, I'd scorn to be a tame eat to any woman an' there's no right-minded woman would ask a man to do it," she concluded, in hot anger. ' "You wouldn't, 'anyway, Jeanie, and Alee's a luck' chap," said Colin, with a touch of envious admiration that quite blinded him to the asperity with which Jeanie referred to Jessie and her doings. Yet though he did not notice it, it was there, and there was a reason for it. Jeanie was one of the young women already referred to, whose ideas tended all in the direction of self-education and imProvement, tendency manifested in nothing more strongly thus her very unusual refinement of speech— unusual for one of her class, that is. Except for an occasional slip, no one could have taken her for one whose tongue's first exercises had been in the fnetory "bat"; and it was to this fastidious choice of language that Jessie had ' referred in calling her, as she did, "Miss rwepa," Thanka tc hcr dead father's tuition,

When he parted from Jeanie at her father's door, all Colin's recent agitation had gone, and he was at least outwardly cool, and even indifferent. But there was a look in his eyes that Jeanie did fmt like, and that stirred in her a momentary doubt whether she had acted wisely in meddling with the matter. On his reaching home his ma,lier was quick to notice that something was wrong, and in answer to her question he told her the whole dismal story, concluding with a wistful request for her advice.

Mrs. Bain's opinion was in entire accord with that of Jeanie Telfer and Alec Glen as to the suspicious nature of Jessie's latest flirtation, but, unlike them' she was unwilling to attach any graver blame to Jessie than the reproach of sheer thoughtlessness. The girl was young—not yet nineteen—she 4:aid, and had had her silly head turned by a host of flattering admirers. In this last instance, that of the man Aylmer, Mrs. Bain was inclined to think that it was only the girl's vanity that had been touched, and had it not been for the proposed excursion to Edinburgh, would have advised Cohn to do no more than look on and exercise patience, in the full certainty that a companionship so ill-assorted could be of no long duration.

"But this Edinburgh business mak's a' the difference in the world," she concluded. The advice thus received furnished Colin with a nple material out of which to construct sit rogramme before he slept, but between con- ' s'xucting programmes and carrying them out Viere is a difference, and, as it turned out, all ha efforts to have speech with Jessie next day wve fruitless. He might a; well have stayed at hiswork' instead of taking Vie day off, for all the good it had dene him. But though so far baffled, he was not at all inclined to accept defeat, now that the necessity fir action had been forced upon him. With but 13 id interval for preparation, he sat out for Govan-street, all else forgotten in an unshakable determination to have this thing ended one way or another.

Govan-street is rather dark at nignt, to which I•eircumstance it was due that in turning into it h 3 Caine into violent collision with a man standing just round the corner of the wall. "Confound you for a blundering idiot!" said the stranger, angrily. "Can't you look where you're going?" Somewhat taken aback, Colin was on the point of offering an apology, when a thought struck him, turning him rigid. The speaker was at lmst approaching middle age, with a sallow, sinister face and heavy black moustache. He was fashionably dressed, and his speech and accent were assuredly not those of a working- man. Who was he? And for whom was he waiting? With the knowledge Colin possessed, to ask the questions was to have them answered, even if the ht anger that surged up in his breast had not blinded him to the possibility of mistake. Sarveying the other from head to foot, he answered, deliberately: "I can always look where I'm going, but sometimes I don't choose to; and it is no worse to be a blundering idiot than it is to be a skulking blackguard." With an ugly oath, Aylmer—for it was he— swung up his cane and aimed a vicious blow at the speaker's face. But the blow was evaded, and its vindictive intent repaid by a stroke on the jaw that sent the assailant staggering back. He recovered himself instantly, however, and a brief but furious struggle ensued between the two men, ending in the elder being thrown heavily to the pavement. Before he could rise to renew the fight, sapposing him to have entertained such inten- tion, there came a quick patter of light feet along the pavement, and Jessie, her blue eyes on fire and her whole lovely face flushed with anger, rushed up. She ' seemed to have no difficulty in apprehending what had happened, for she turned on Colin like a fury and gave him sueh an estimate of himself as on the instant extinguished the last hope of reconcilia- tion to which he had hitherto clung. Then deliberately, ostentatiously even, she addressed Ayhner, all sympathy and concern. lI e hope, he began. es,ldo hope, you are not hurt, Mr. Aylmer," "Oh, it is nothing at all, Miss French," he replied, hastily. "I was foolish to take any notice of the ruffian—ought to have handed him over to the police. Pray do not mind it, Miss French. It is only an unpleasant trifle, after all."

And with the daintily-dressed little Spire a7,eompanying him, the .speaker walked off. Hick a" heart out of sheer despairing .helplessness, Calin stood looking after them for a space, and was then turning dejectedly away when his gianee fell upon a white object on the pavement —plainly a letter fallen from Aylmer's pocket in Vic recent struggle. With an exclamation he Iv-Latched it up, and, hastening to the nearest lull?, examined it breathlessly. The post-mark was that of Edinburgh, the date two days previously, while the name on the els-elope was not Aylmer, but Dibb—Finlay Dibb, to give it in full. The address was Rose- s;reet, Garnet Hill, and having noted that, Colin, his hands trembling with excitement, took out tie enclosed sheet and read it. it was interesting reading, too, seeing that in it one boon companion in Edinburgh eongratu- I tted another in Glasgow on being able to get a%Nity for once without his "Old Dutch," promised him the highest of high old times during his forthcoming visit, and—most important of all, from Colin's point of view—assured him that while the "little pick-up, Jessie French," was in evidence, he, the writer, would not forget that the name was not Finlay Dibb, but John Aylmer. As he ended the perusal of this precious epistle, Colin drew a long breath, smiling g imly in spite a his anger and disgust. Then, as he realised how tremendous was the woapon thus providentially furnished, he laughed

"Well, this should about settle you, confound s-qii!" he muticred, apostrophising the absent Aylmer. "Wonder 1-43 w Jessie would look if I w.tre to slim her this and ask her how she likes bring a married man's pick-up! But that would downright cruelty-- -and as there's no time to lose, l'll go up to llose-street and see that B .oundrel's wife. I only hope she's one of the wild-cat sort, for his salse. "

His hope Was not long in reaching the stage of ewtainty, although there was less of the wild- cit than the tigress in the finding. She was a tall, bony women, a good deal &der than her husband, and her hard-favoured Uwe and cold grey eyes were the living embodi- ments of gnawing disappointment, harsh sus- picion, and, underlying both, a sort of grimly pithetie patience, as that of one who waits in a wilderness of weariness for the coming of an gild of which the whole is known. Colin had in him nothing of the art of the diplomatist, and came st-aight to the point, telling his story from first to last, with neither minimising on the one baud, nor exaggeration on Vie other. She listened in silence but the face that had been only severe when he began had grown genuinely terrible by the time he had finished. It was a thing darkly significant, too, of past experiences, that she never once seemed

The Examiner, Saturday, January 13, 1906.

"You have saved that foolish girl, Mr. Bain, and for that your conscience will reward you," she said. "But you have also done me a service, and for that I thank you. This man, my husband, is a coward, a liar, and worse—how much worse you may judge when I say that I, his wife, whom he professed to love but whom he really married for her money, that I will no longer stand between him and—and that to which he has sentenced himself. Twice, thrice he has flung my. heart under his feet and trampled on it. He shall do so no more." Her voice had sunk to a whisper, and seeing that she had forgotten his presence, Colin experienced a thrill of very real uneasiness. This woman, with her tragic face and awful eyes, was in very truth a being to shudder at— one, he dimly felt, easily capable of passing beyond humanity and becoming Send. "Well, I will move in this matter to-morrow, and you need not fear for the issue as effecting Mies French," she said, rousing herself from her bitter abstraction. "Like you, I think she has been no worse than foolish, and as I made the same mistake once, lam not disposed to judge her harshly. I will keep this letter; you will give me the address of Miss French, as also your own, in ease I may have occasion to write to you. I will call on Miss French to-morrow night, taking Mr. Dibb with me, and--" "Will he go ? " asked Colin, involuntarily interrupting. She looked at him, and the smile on her face made him shiver.

"He will go," she said, quietly. "He has not a penny in the world but what I give him. That is one reason—and there are others. In her mother's presence r will shew Miss French that letter, tell her the truth, and compel him to attest it. Afterwards-- But that does not concern you, Now write down the a4dresses, and then please go." He obeyed, and went out in silence, his heart aching for the still misery of this stony woman, even while the indefinable dread with which sh 6 had from the first inspired him grew and deepened; dread of something impending—he knew not what.

The following day was an anxious one for Colin. He did not once go near the factory., but from Jeanie, whom, with Alec, he saw in the earlier part of the evening, he learnt that Jessie kad been in high spirits all day over her pro- jected excursion. To them he related the events of the previous night, his discovery affecting Aylmer, and the action decided upon by the latter's wife. Asked what he himself intended to do, he replied that in the meantime he could only await developments, and after Jessie had had time to recover from the shock. approach her again and endeavour to regain his foruul position. His hearers agreed that such would bf the wisest course for him to follow; but whec: they had parted from him, Jeanie turned to her lover, her fine face all alight with eager sy!fipathy. I want you to come over to Covan-atreet with me' Alec," she said. "The Dibbs will be sure to have gone by nine, so that I'll see Jessie alone."

"But " began Alec, in bewilderment. "You silly! What if we haven't been friendly before? Do you think I don't feel for her in such trouble as this—the shame, the humiliation, the fear? I want to see her, to tell her that nobody really knows anything, but only U 8 three —you, Cohn, an' myself—and get her to come out to-morrow as usual, so that there won't be any talk worth botherin' about. Besides, I'd like to put in a word for poor Colin. Wye ne see ? " _

--- - "Yes, ye kind-hearted darling, I do see," said Alec, with emphasis, and, regardless of who might be looking, he caught her round the waist a„a kissed her.

"Alec Glen ! On the public street!" exclaimed Jeanie, in horror; which horror, however, the eyes with which she looked up at her tall lover discounted heavily. Next day Jessie appeared at the factory, 'ooking very pale, but cbtherwise shewing no ign of the terrible experience she had passed ahrough. It was noticed, too, that she and Jeanie Telfer had become very good friends all at once, and the reason ai'orded room for a good deal of speculation, as RIG° did the sudden abandonment of the much-talked-of trip to Edinburgh. It might have been noticed, too, had the necessary keenness of observation been forthcoming, that she seemed to be all day afraid of meeting someone, for she hurried in and out a the factory during the day, and quitted its vicinity at nightfall with a haste altogether unusual. But Colin—for it was a meeting with him she feared—was not minded to risk further misunderstanding, and only when she was safely in her home did he seek her. From Jeanie he had learnt something of the agony of shame and distress in which she had found Jessie, and the one thought in his heart as he approached the house was how he would atone to her for both— just as if the fault had been his, to begin with. When he entered the room where she sat, she started up ; her face alternately red and white, and in the fevered blue eyes a look that went to his very heart. In an instant he was across the room and had her in his arms, while she clung to him, trembling, and tried ta speak. "Colin, I—l-- He lifted the piteous little face, enforcing silence with a kiss. "That is past, dear," he said. "Forget it." At that, the last vestige of self-restraint left her, and the tears came like a torrent. It .was more than an hour before the storm spent itself, and with the peace that followed, Aylmei, or rather, Dibb, was forgotten. But not for long—no longer than four dap; afterwards, when Colin. with a sick horror gathering at his heart, read in the newspapers a.n Necount of the death of Finlay Dibb by accidental poisoning. "A distressing fatality, the report called it, and everything in the account pointed to misadventure on the part of the deed man. It might have been so, the young man thought, even remembering the face and words of the woman that night, it might have been so, but— [THE END.]

Wycliffe P.S!A. Society.

Theamml meeting in Connection with the War- rington P.S.A. Society was held on Sunday. Mr. E. Wynne Jones presiding. The Secretary presented Ids report, which showed that the membership now stands at 297. Councillor Clegg proposed a vote of thanks to the officers and all'werkers for what they had done during the year, making special reference to the service that had been rendered to the society bv the President and Mr. B. Wadsworth. The resolution was carried. Mr. E. Wynne Jones was unanimously ' re-elected president. The following officers werc also elected :—Vice-president, Mr. it. Nelson ; treasurer, Mr. T. Garnet ; joint secretaries, Messrs. J. D. Gibson and P. Bendy: with Mr. A. Parr assistant. A strong committee of IS members was also elected.

A small ketch, on which three men could be distinctly seen, sank in full view of the staff of St. Catherine's lighthouse on Tuesday. Alifebuoy, bearing the name "Rolla, Bridgewater,' has since been cast ashore. Mary Walker and Helen Pearson were on Tuesday committed to Berkshire Assizes on a charge of stealing £250 worth of :jewels belonging to Mrs. Richard Chamberlain, of Lode Court, Abingdon, sister-in-law of the ex-Colonial Secretary.

POISONED BY COLD WEATHER. An Unsuspected Winter Danger Revealed.

Everybody knows how unpleasant is a thorougl: chill, the cold that seems to grip your heartstrings and make you feel physically sick ; but that a severe chill is capable of, poisoning the body is an assertion as true as it is startling. The poisoning spoken of is effected in this foshion, To repel the attack of cold, blood rushes from the vital Organs outwards to the skin and muscles, and much of the vital heat is lost in the effort. The liver is called upon for increased supplies of food-laden blood, and new supplies of vital heat, and yetis robbed one-half its own supply of bl•od. Every such chill affects the liver, with the inevitable result that the process of filtering poisons from the food-juices, which is one of the most important functions of the liver, is partly suspended. These poisons escape into the blood, causing bilious attacks, indigestion, palpitation, sick headache, and other familiar evils of the digestive organs. Warrington v. Leeds.

The werther conditions et Warrington were of the worst on Saturday, rain falling continuously, and a high wind blowing from the nerth-west. Only about 1.500 spectators assembler' to witness the game between War- rington and Leeds. bat they were rewarded he an excel- lently contested game, in spite of the mud. Warrington were short-banded in the back division. Fish and Hockenhall crving-off at the last minute. trbiie Leeds brought a full team, including J. Hughes, late of Widnes. Teams : Warrington :—Knight, back ; Widdeson, ;Tones. Ken- yon, and Harris.three-nuarter becks ; Lloyd and Davies half backs : Preston. Boardman. Shugars, Belton,Swift

... . _ Thomas, Heath and Harmer. forwards. Leeds :,—Naylor, hack ; Jenkins,Thomas,T. Llewellyn and linvhes, threo-ouarter backs ; WArd and Mosley, half backs ; Howlett, Watts,Webster,Wainwright,Birch, Lunn, Crampton and Wormald, forwards. Referee, Mr. Robinson, Afannincham. Leeds had the assistance of a strong wind during the flrst half, and early on Hughes, the new wing three- quarter, CAMP very near scoring. Knight only saving by taking a running kick into touch. The home line was also seriously endangered, by a dribble started by Mosley, but Lloyd on this occasion CAMP to the rescue, and cleared his line. Warrington were penalised for a scrummage informality. and Leeds pressed, but Kenyon drove them hack, and Leeds in turn had to defend. Naylor kicked out. of danger. and a mistake by Knight almost let the visitors in. Leeds had the best of the fltst half, but inst before the interval the visiting backs were several times called upon to stop rushes by Pres- ton and his crew. On one occasion Swift reached within a yard of the line, and it remained for Jenkins to fall on the ball to Rave a score. .

With nothing on the score sheet up to half-time the advantage was transferred to Warrington. who at once began to attack. lTnches not the visiting side out, of danger twice, but the " Wirepullers " were not to he j denied, and ICenvon eventually scored as the result, of a forward msh. which Llewellyn, Thomas, and Naylor all failed in turn to check. The try was converted by Preston. With a five Points lead the "Wirepuller; " clearly had the best of matters. and within the next five minutes, after continually bombarding the Leeds line, they scored another try, which was credited to Preston, who, however, was unable to add the goal Points. Llewellyn and Jones made a good run before the close, but there was no further scoring, and the game then ended with the result :—Warrington 1 goal 2 tries (8 points), Leeds nil.

"MR. VOTER."

(With acknowledgments to Mr. Kipling.) I went up there to Parliament, and said I'd called

about The promises they'd made to me, and hadn't carried

out. The bobby at the doorway Lirfed—he laded 'until he

cried, "it ain't no use you comin"ere," sea le. ontside.". Yes, it's " Put that man outside! Move on! Confound you, go away ! " But it's "Step into my motor !" when it comes to Polling Day. It's " Sir " on Polling Day, my boys, it's " Sir " on Polling Day— And it's" Thank you, Mt. Voter," when it, comes to Polling Day. I went to Mr. C'nanibarlain, and said I'd called to know About that Old-Age Pension promised me some time

"So get

ago. For I haven't got my pension, nor Three Acres and a Cow. But it isn't "Old Age Pensions "—it's "Our Gloricius Empire "now ! It's" Empire "this, and "Empire "that, and "Pay- pay-pay ! " galore But your Empire is the workhouse when you can't work any more. When you can't work any more, my boys, you can't work any more; You will be a " bloomin pauper" when you can't work any more. I went ta Mr. Balfour (he looked very much annoyed). About some hungry chums of Mine among the unem- ployed. Sez I " They're sons of Empire—and they're, every one."'

Sez he "It's no good comin' here, for nothing can be done." It's doles for 'high-class friends, my boys, it's doles for squire and lord. But a dole for hungry workers is a dole they can't i afford. A dale they can't afford, my boys.... but there's the casaal ward— And stones to break, and skilly when you reach the casual ward. I paid a lot of tames (and I'm pay-pay-payin still) For the planting of the Union Jack upan Pretoria hill. An' I cheered for Joe and Khaki. " ! " sez • Joe. " it will be grand When in proud imperial splendour free-boca Britons rule the Rand !" It was "Thank you, Mr. Atkins !" when 'é sent our boys to die. But now it's Chinese tabour, and—no British need apply." "Thank you, Mr. Briton, you can pay, and fight, and But when it's work and wealth, my boys—No British need apply."

They nromise you Prosperity, More Work, and lots of tin, But it's Waste and Mess and Muddle when you've put the beggars in. Now it's got a bran-new bottom—their electioneering tub, Ho ! " Let us make you rich by putting taxes on your grub." _

THANKS TO CUTICURA IT IS ALL OVER NOW

"I was troubled with sore hands, so 'sore that when I would put them in water the pain would nearly sit me crazy, the skin would peel off and the flesh get hard and break, with blood flowing from atleast fifty places on each hand. Words could never tell the suf- fering I endured for three years. I tried eight different doctors' but none did me any good, as my hands were as bad as when I began. I also tried many reme- dies, but none of them ever did me one cent's worth of good. I was discouraged and heart-sore. I would feel so bad mornings, to think I had to go to work and stand the pain for ten hours' I often felt like giving up my position. I would. have to wrap every finger up separately, so as to try, and keep them soft, and then wear gloves over the rags to keep the grease from getting on my work. I had to wear gloves at night; in fact, all the time. But thanks to Cuticura, that is all over now. After doctoring for three years, and spending much money, a single box of Cuticura Ointment ended all my sufferings. It's been two years since I used any, and I don't know what sore hands are now, and never lost a day's work while using Cuticura. Thomas A. Clancy, 310 North Montgomery St., Trenton, N. J."

THE EXAMINER.

JANUARY 13th 1906.

For many Liberals and THE CHANCE OF Progressives in Warring- A LIFE-TIME, ton the polling on Mon- day will afford the chance of a life-time. It is nearly 26 years since a Liberal was returned for Warrington in the porton of the late John Gordon McMinnies, and in the long weary wait since those glorious days there has been very little sign of light in the outer darkness through which Warrington has stumbled. This is the sixth occasion on which there has been an appeal to the electorate in these twenty-five years and while the hypes of Liberals have ru n high on several occasions, there was never a time when we had such unity in the party, such whole•hearted enthusiasm, and such solid grounds for confidence in a satisfactory iss‘le. The previous election at which our present champion, Mr. A. H. Crosfield, entered the lists for the first time,is fortunately not a fair criterion of the strength of the parties in War- rington. On that occasion, just over five years ago, the Tory majority rose o the high figure of 1165,but then there were exceptional causes which cannot be gainsaid, and which are non-existent to day. In fact the position of affairs, as reflected by public opinion, is exactly reversed. In 1900 we had reached a critical stage in the South African war; the Tory Government of the day had taken a favourable opportunity of appealing to the country for a mandate to push the conflict in South Africa to a conclusion, and that mandate could not be refused even by many people who had been uncompromisingly hostile to a resort to arms from the very first. it was a case of "in for a penny in for a pound ;"—the war had to be finished, and the vote of the people was given for reasons very largely ind ependent of party politics.

The normal Conservative majority in War- rington may be put down at 600. We will endeavour to show briefly why we conclude that that majority will be turned into a minority on Monday. In the first place it only requires the transference of some 300 Conservative votes to the Liberal side to wipe out the 600. Under ordinary circumstances it would be as reasonable to wish for the moon as to expect even 309 Tories Co vote Liberal in a War- rington Parliamentary contest. But the circum- stances era extra-ordinary. Mr. Chamberlain has split the nation on his Protectionist plea, and this is not a party question. Protection is as bad for Tories as for Liberals, and many of them, as half-a hundred bye-elections have shown, are well aware of this. There are many Tories who will not have Protection at any price, and these men may be expected to range themlelves along with the solid ' phalanx of Liberal Free Traders. That there are three hundred of them in Warrington, is not, we think an unreasonable asstunption.But should they number only half 300, there are other and weighty reasons why the Liberal vote should be swelled to a considerable extent. The last ten years of Tory Government have been remarkable for the heavy call they have made upon the public purse. Rates and taxes have reached an alarmingly high fiuure, and a change of Government with its possibilities of economy is imperative. Then there is the Chinese Labour ordinance which has disgusted the British workers from one end of the Kingdom to I the other. They contributed £250,000,000 and thousands of lives towards the settlement of South African affairs, only to discover that the cosmo- politan financiers of the Rand had got the Govern- met of this country under their thumb, and when they demanded imported coolie labour at their own wage per day they got it, in the shape of 47,000 Chinese indentured under conditions for which slavery is the only word conveying an honest description. Moreover the Chinese have displaced both the native Kaffir and the white miners. Neither "need apply. No wonder the workingmen of this country are strongly in favour of the new administration which has already stopped the importation of fresh Chinese and bids fair to free South Africa from the very real horrors of the" Yellow peril." Then

there is—as an additional local factor of import- ance—what is known as the "floating vote," which is always carried by the "flowing tide." The direction of the latter is unmistakable at the present moment, and the votes of those mortals who like to feel that they are at one with the bulk of the people of the country should be cast entirely for the Liberal candidate. There is no doubt whatever that Mr. Crosfield should win this time, and we believe be will. One danger alone do we fear, and that is that a number of the unlettered workers in Warrington—more particularly the ironworkers—may be led astray by the sophistries of selfish masters who put personal pecuniary gain before all considerations affecting the national well-being. The men are being promised more w 3rk and higher wages. More work they might have, for a brief period under Protection, but the higher wages they must class with other election promises of a notorious frailty. What it is vitally important they should not forget is this: that while they were getting more work, the price of food and other necessaries would rise until very soon they would be poorer than before. The next development would be a reduction in the output by the forges and manufactories, conse- quent on a readjustment of conditions under cut- throat tariff competition. Work and wages would be reduced, but the cost of living would remain as high as ever, and the dark days of the " forties " would have dawned again. Working- men,we say, shun Tariff Reform and Protection, as you would the wiles of the Evil One. They are singularly similar, and equally ruinous. Instead give a thumping majority to CROSFIELD,

===== 0002601_19060113_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
FREE TRADE, CHEAP FOOD, AND No SLivEitY

Apart from his staunch Tun Wroaxtriamax's adherence to Free Trade CANDIDATE. and his unsparing con- demnation of Chinese labour, there are many other very strong reasons why Mr. Crostield is entitled to the support of workingmen. His views on important trade union questions meet with the approval of the representatives of the Parliamentary Committee of the Trade Union Congress and—what is of vital concern to workingmen—Mr. Crosfield has publicly declared himself in favour of a removal of the disabilities which judge-made law has imposed ' on trade unions. The difficult problem of the unem- ployed is one to which the Liberal candidate has given earnest consideration, and whatever may be the ultimate solution, " work, not charity," must be the guiding principle. Mr. Crosfield is a most ardent advocate of the taxation of land values— 1 and particularly of site values—a great reform which would undoubtedly do much to remedy unemployment and tend to the ameliora- tion of the lot of the masses in many other directions. It is by such internal „ improvements as this, and not by attempting to tax the foreigner that we shall help to make a „ happier England. The Liberals have a magnificent ! programme of social progress and a splendid Cabinet—in which for the first time Labour has a direct representative—to carry it into effiict. If the workingmen of Warrington wish to further their own interests they will vote solidly for Mr. Crosfield!

It is not very long, as MR. F. MONKS IN political conversions —or 1891 AND Now. perversions—go, since no one in Warrington would have questioned the Liberal and Free Trade beliefs of Mr. F. Monks. To-day there is no doubt of the fact that Mr. Monks is a Protectionist, and many of his old Warrington friends fear that he is a Tory also, seeing that he has this week been billed to appear on a Tory platform in support of Mr. R. Paerpoint. Yet we are sure that Mr. Monks will resent being dubbed "Tory." He has not been across the border-line long enough to be ' thoroughly saturated with the " true blue " of Conservatism, and only a fortnight ago he avowed himself "as good a Liberal as ever, and a thorough Free Trader." Etther Mr. Monks has ' forgotten the meaning of plain words, or he is in a very uncomfortable and nebulous political state. A man must, however, be judged by his actions rather than by his words,and Mr. Monks' actions forbid Liberals from claiming him as one of Elemselves. Only a month ago Mr. Monks put questions publicly to both the candi dates for the Borough, the answers to which must have been as obvious to himself as to everybody else. ' He knew Mr. Crosfield was a Free Trader,' and that his opponent was a Protectionist. Why inquire, therefore, through the public Press ? Mr. Crosfield in his reply asked some pertinent ques- tions which provoked from Mr. Monks the avowal. quoted above. Since then Mr. Monks has appar entlylr one over." How he can claim to be a " Free Trader" when "Free Trade" has only one I accepted meaning, we are at a loss to understand, At any rate his views as a Free Trader in 1891 ctiffer very much from those he expresses now. 1 Below we make a comparison of his utterances, which will afford interesting reading to those 1 people who have been Free Traders of one kind , only all their lives:

PtERPOINT AND answers which have THR NON-TRADERS. passed between the candi dates and the Non-Trad- ing Association, and which we publish in another column, our readers will discover that both Mr. Crosfield and ,Mr. Pierpoint fully recognize the importance of the agitation which has taken place in Warrington during the last few years, and, furthermore, promise to vote for an amendment in the law such as would make illegal the trading done by individuals through limited companies with public bodies of which they are members. A circular issued by the Non-Traders expresses satisfaction that " both candidates are in entire harmony with the principles for which we have fought for the last ' five or six years." This is, perhaps, the great- est triumph which the Non-Traders have gainsd and affirms in striking fashion the justice and morality of the principle that no man shall obtain private pecuniary advantage from his public position. This is the first public pronouncement or oxpres sion tf opinion by either candidate on the Non- Trading question. For once in a way, the THE WATER monthly Town Council COMMITTEE'S meetieg„ on Tuesday even- LATEST SHUFFLE. ing, was a matter of small public concern. Local affairs are dwarfed by the great national issues at : stake, and people have almost forgotten that the , Town Council has a lot to do with their well-being and personal comfort. Nevertheless a few of the critics were in their regular places, and for two hours they had plenty to criticise. The Council was not at its best, and to make matters worse much of the business was of the parish pump order resulting in discussions rernark,b,e for their trivi- ality. There was one important item on the minutes of the Water Committee relative to the appointment of an arbitrator in the dispute between the Corporation and Mr. LIWSOD, con- tractor. Seeing that the Corporation is already committed to an expenditure of some hundreds of pounds in the employment of Mr. Garside, C.E., to report on the work done under the disputed contract, and that he has not yet submitted his findings, the appointment of an arbitrator at this stage seemed a remarkable course to adopt. Opposition of the most strenuous nature was inevitable and there was small probability of the resolution being confirmed. So the Water Com- mittee did a hurried shuffle the day before the Council meeting and "caved in." 'When the minutes came up Alderman Francomb withdrew the one iu question, though it was printed on the agenda and had not been discti&ied. It was obvious that the Committee dreaded a discussion, i and their sudden right-about-face caused the , . raising of a knotty point of order which the Mayor failed to properly elucidate. One of the most remarkable things about Warrington's corporate managemeet is the way in which the Water Committee comes into conflict with most people's ideas as to what is proper and business- like. It has an unhappy knack of doing the wrong thing at the wrong time.

* * * The saying " Once a Tory always a Tory" is, in this General Election, being proved fallacious, as Mr. Robert Pierpoint is finding out to his discomfiture. * * * "'Vote for the Conservative candidate and the- :Union," is one of the Tory cries. We say : " Vote for the Free Trade candidate, and keep out of the Union."

* * * It would be well for us to remember that whilst I Monday next is Election Day yet the world does not come to an end then. Let the fight be fought ia such a manner that friends may still be friends anit let nothing be done which will give cause for regret in the future. *** A good story is credited to Mr. Lloyd-George. At one of his campaign meetings in the Carnarvog Boroughs a heckler, with a view of disparaging his origin, asked him if he remembered his grand- ' father driving a donkey and cart. "You will have to forgive me, ladies and gentlemen," was the witt. reply," but the cart has quite escaped my memory I see the donkey is still alive." * *

Have you a fixed salary If you have—what think you of Protection? The man with a fixed salary has everything to fear and nothing to hope from so-called "tariff reform." Even if the other people got more work prices would rise, and the " fixed salary ' would have less purchasing power. Cheapness in food, clothing, manufactures,education —in everything—is the nation's only hope of salvation, and it is only possible under Free Trade. *s* " Joey " speaking at Tunstall on the :27th Septem- ber, 1900 said : "How is it that in England, Scotland and Ireland you have 40 millions of people, most of them artisans and workmen, who are more prosperous at any rate, than working men are in any other portion of Europe? Ido not say that your csndition may not be improved, but I say without doubt at the present it is infinitely better than in any Continental nation "—What has our friend Mr. R. Pierpoint—the pro-boar or rather whole-hogger to say in reply to that: statement ?

* * * The outrages perpetuated by Chinese on the rand. are continuing to such an extent that Transvaal residents are providing themselves with fire arms ag a means of protection. Between December 11 and December IGth, reports are to band of a farmer 'being shot and a miner's wife maltreated, of two Chinese. policemen hacked to pieces, of a Chinese miner found with his throat cut,ancl of an Indian mortally wounded, For so short a period the record has scarcely been surpassed since the outrages first began. Ale.anwhilo dealers in revolvers and other fire arms are sold out.

* It is unfortunate for Protectionists that in Germany the Place of high tariffs, the lot of the workingman does not compare at all favourably with the conditions enjoyed in England. At the meeting held at Orford 'n support of Mr. Ctosfields' candidature, the audience had an opportunity of seeing a spacimea of the bread generally eaten by the working classes of Germany. It had been " specially impried " for the occasion, but it did not look at all inviting, and no one seemed inclined to taste it. Englishmen prefer the Free Trade loaf ! .

* Mr. R .A. Naylor, the Conservative candidate for Carnarvon Boroughs, has recently experienced a new form of interruption. Addressing his Constituents on Tuesday evening, in a building roofed over with corrugated iron, his utterances were at one stage drowned by some one throwing stones on the roof, Mr. Naylor regarded the incident lightly and said "t What he might yet have to face he did not know, but he had been threatened, and he was prepared to ran the racket if he could do good to the working classes."

At the Police Court this (Friday) morning, Edmond Green Barratt, Blackpool, formerly carrying on busi- ness as a wholesale biscuit merchant and confectioner at Warrington was charged with serious offences under three sections of the Debtor's Act, 1869—Mr. Rylands, of Manchester, instructed by the official receiver, prosecuted and Mr Steel defended - Prisoner was formally remanded until Wednesday, it being understood that afurther remand will then be applied for.—Mr. Steel applied for bail, pointing out that prisoner had been engaged as an insurance agent at Blackpool and although he knew proceedings were being taken made no attempt to abscond or to conceal his address.—Bail was allowed, prisoner in £lOO and two other sureties of £lOO each.

The members of the debating section of the above flourishing society met the Liverpool Catholic Young Men's Society at Warrington on Thursday evening in the third round of the Debating Shield Competition, open to the young men's societies of the country. The subject was Should arlntration in trades disputes be enforced by law." The Warrington Society took the affirmative, tho debate Using opened by Mr. Owen Toole. Petroleum Oil in barrels and drums delivered free.—J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, bridge st., Warring- ton.—Advt. Poor Children's Treats. The seventh annual treat to Warrington's poor children was held in the Parr Hall, on Thursday,when over 1,200 youngsters were splendidly entertained. Mr. James Monroe again provided the tea and full justice was done to an excellent repast. The wants of the children were superintended by the ladies' com- mittee, consisting of Mrs. W. J. Forshaw, Mrs. James Richardson, Mrs. Clegg, the Misses Clegg, the Misses Gill, Miss Gibson, Miss Bowcock, Mrs. Robert Pier- point, Miss Bolton, Lady Greenall, and Miss Crosfield. After tea an entertainment, which was thoroughly enjoyed, was given by children from People's College. "The fire brigade" and "Mulligan's guard" were given by chorus of boys; C. Ellam gave " Three for Jack " and Ernest Wallington sang with great success " Ooperzootic." Dances were given by the Misses May and Madge Woolstencroft, Miss Agitha Jones and Master E. Woolstencroft. Every item was vociferously encored. Daring the evening oranges and toys were distributed amongst the children who by hearty cheers expressed their delight at the efforts towards their entertainment. Miss Smith and Mr. W. H. Payton acted as accompanists. Much of the success of the proceedings was due to the. efforts of Mr. Henry Taylor and Mr. W. ILL ,Tenkins,who acted res- pectively as hon. secretary and lion, treasurer of the treat.—About 550 of the poorest children in the districts of Orford-lane, Winwick-road, and Bewsey, were on Saturday last entertained in the Silver-street Sunday school, by the Christmas carol singers. This was the third annual event arranged by the singers and was highly successful. After a hot-pot which was served, a short concert, presided over by the Rev. S. Pitt was held, the chief items which were the selec- tions on the phonograph, by Mr. H. Whitney. At the close, each child was presented with a has containing a mince pie, cake and orange.

WEEKLY REPORT ON THE HEALTH OF WARRINGTON.

WEEK ENDING JANUARY 6Th, 1906. During the week, the following births and deaths were registered :—Warrington Registration District births, GO, deaths, 26. The eicess of births over deaths was 34. The birth-rate was equal to 451. The death-rate from all causes was equal to 19'5 per 1,000 against 16.4 the event ge of the previous 4 weeks and against 220 the corresponding period of last year. The death rate from the seven chief zymotic diseases was equal to 1-5 per 1,000, against PG the average rate of the previous 4 weeks and against 15 the corresponding period of 1905. The ages of mortality were :- 10-1 1-5 5-15 15-25 25-65 65—up. Total 8 3 8 7 26. The deaths included-2 from whooping cough, 1 other tubercular diseases, 2 from cancer, G from bronchitis,3 from premature birth,2 from heart diseases, 110 from all other causes. The following cases of infectious diseases have been reported during the week :—Scarlet fever 2 cases, Membranous -cramp.
