The Examiner, Saturday, January 13, 1906. LETTERS TO THE EDITOR. THE EVE OF THE POLL. LATEST PARTICULARS AND ARRANGEMENTS FOR MONDAY.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

[Several letters to the Editor will be foam/ on page G.] G. N. VERNON.—We regret that, owing to the great quantity of correspondence, we are unable to find room for your letter. Ed. " Ex." G. VALE OwEs.—Re Church Society's Questions, You will find a complete answer from Air. A. H. Cros- field in the printed circular which has been issued by Rev. H. A. Lester, (hon. secretary ‘Varrington Church Society).

1 What a Conservative Thinks of the Fiscal Policy. Sir,—As a workingman who has hitherto supported the Conservative party I crave the indulgence of your I space to protest against the proposed Fiscal Policy. I ' think I urn at the present time in command of brain power sufficient to enable me to know what is to my benefit and the reverse, and hope the majority of my class possess the same knowledge, and will not be mis- led by empty and contradictory promises. Of course Mr. Chamberlain paints his case in glowing colours, but as a workingman on whose weekly wage depends the health and happiness of myself and family, I wish to place my way of looking at the proposed change of policy before your readers. In the first place, beyond all contradiction, we have prospered under Free Trade, as any person who will take the trouble to look up Board of Trade figures can verify beyond doubt- Now if as Mr. Chamberlain asserts we are threatened by foreign competition why amongst all our learned men and politicians is Mr. Chamberlain the only person to see the danger and why should he be the first person to bring it before the public? If the danger exists why is not the whole Conservative party trying to convert the country or make obvious to us the danger? They profess to be the friends of the workingmen and if we are in this grave danger they are shirking their duties. Does not the fact that this policy is the hobby of one man prove that there is a doubt in the case. To my mind it does. Is Mr. Chamberlain a man whom we can trust to lead us into a change of policy which is of vital interest to the country ? Judging from his change of parties and opinions certainly not. Now he says "You must put a tax on food? Moreover, if the price of food is raised the rate of wages will certainly be raised in greater proportion" (June 1903). Speaking in the liouse of Commons in 1881 he said" A tax on food will mean a decline in wages," also that "under the sting of great suffering and eleccirect bt, misrepresentations the working classes might be willing to try strange remedies and fcotisk enough to submit to a proposal to tax the food of the country." How does he reconcile these state- ments? Again on the subject of old age pensions he contradicts himself direct. Either a case of bad memory or a great disregard of the truth. ITo look at the matter locally I append extracts from the " Observer " when our Conservative member asked for our votes at the last General Election. It is very interesting reading. All was then well : £40,398,500 more standing to the credit of De- positors in Sowings Banks in the United Kingdom at the end of 1393 compared with the end of 1894. £8,752,230 more were spent in the working expenses of British and Irish Railways, and of this a large sum was added to the wages of Railway Employees. 33,386 more Marriages in the United Kingdom. 14,000 fewer able-bodied Paupers in England and Wales. 514 fewer Bankruptcies in Great Britain in 1898 —and so on.

We had apparently then not commenced to go to the dogs. Now is it commonsense that in the brief period of live years we have lost our place among the nations, or that there is any need for the change they suggest? Why this great change in so brief a period. In 1900 we were the most prosperous nation and all that was necessary to maintain that position was to support Mr. Pierpoint. Now we are effete and old-fashioned and only the ' great (if changeable) brain of Mr. Chamberlain can ex- tricate us. Vorkingmen think for yourselves ! Is not the manner in which this fiscal programme is placed , before the country—i.e.—not by one of the great parties but by a single politician—suggestive of something behind the scenes. If the danger existed we have men with as great or greater brain capacity than" J.C." who would have spoken fearlessly on both sides of the house. Of that am convinced and on that account although a Conservative on principle have decided to do all one man can do to defeat this change of policy, hence this letter. I especially appeal to those workingmen who like myself are Conservative but above all Free Traders." Sink party feeling on this great national question and support even a Radical to defeat this deliberate attempt to hoodwink the workers of the country .—I am, yours etc., PRO BONO PUBLIC 3.

Monday will witness the most momentous election in the political history of Warrington. The electors will be called upon to give their verdict on the great issue, which touches every worker's home in the country, of Free Trade v. Protection. That the verdict ' will be overwhelmingly against the Protective policy, which would involve the taxation of necessaries of life and imperil our commercial prosperity, we are fully con- fident. Never before has there been such remarkable enthusiasm in the progressive ranks. The workers are throwing themselves into the campaign with the determination to win. "Crosfield for Warrington is the rallying cry, and the magnificent response has exceeded all anticipations. The past week has bee one of unparallelled enthusiasm and activity, and in every ward the candidate has had splendid recep- tions. It is quite evident that the electors are fully alive to the magnitude of the issues and that they intend to return Mr.Cro3field triumphantly on Monday.. The Free Trade League—a non-party organization which provides a common platform for all who believe 1 in Free Trade —is strongly urging the electors to vote for Mr. Crostield, whose answers to the questions sub• mitted to him were considered eminently satisfactory Several !."delegates from the Lufgue have attended each meeting this week and given the advice to men of all parties to " vote for Crosfield and Free Trade." A great quantity of literature has been circulated in the constituency, and from an educational point of view the Liberals have left no stone unturned. Party favours are being freely worn, and in the windows of hundreds of houses cards bearing Mr. Crosfield's photograph, and the Free Trade and Protectionist loaves, together with a little comparison of the con- ditions of life in England and Germany, are displayed. The hoardings are covered with election matter, and a poster recording Mr. Piernoint's adverse votes on labour questions has attracted considerable attention.

South Africa. (Cheers) With regard to the action of the new Liberal Gievernment, it must be borne in mind that the 50,000 Chinese already on the Rand were bound by indentures fora period of years. It would not be easy to displace them by a stroke of the pen and it would be extremely costly to thecountry to compensate the wealthy randlords. As businessmen,all the Liberal Government at present could do was to see that no more Chinese went there (Cheers). Referring to the !fiscal question, Mr. Roberts saw their opponents held that we did not get a fair proportion of the trade of the world. Were they aware that the export trade of manufactured goods for the last year on which the comparative figures were got out 1902 was as fol- lows :—United States £54,000,000 ; France £95,000,000; , Germany £154,000,000; England £233,ooo,ooo.(Cheers).iu the same period our shipping was five times greater than that from Germanyteight times greater than from Prance more than twelve times as much as the United States. (Cheers). One of the effects of Protection, continued Mr. Crosfield was to restrict individual enterprise and inventive faculty. The corruption which followed the combination of business firms under Protection was un- speakable. Alluding to the advantages of Free Trade to British industries,Mr.Roberts said they had in that ward a large and growing industry which, if it were not for the competition which kept the price of iron rods below a certain figure, would be wiped out altogether. " What is raw material ?" was the posing question which the tariff reformers had never answered yet. Mr. Roberts showed how even a 2s. duty on corn would probably raise the price of the 41b loaf by Ad. and that Protection would increase the cost of living all round. The pro- gressives, he concluded, had a splendid cause when they said to the Tory party " Whatever else you tax, you don't tax the food of the poor." (Cheers.)

A Protectionist Argument Rebutted,

Sir,—l was a privileged member of the large audience which assembled in the Parr Hall last night and though my expectations were not fully gratified I will not com- plain of the menu. I .heard Mr. Pierpoint, whom I have heard many times before, at his best. It was certainly a very great improvement on his performance at the great Conservative meeting addressed by Mr. A. Bonar Law. But even last night's address was not with- out its disappointment to me, For the great issue of this election and the great question of the hour in which I and the electorate generally are most interested appeared to he studiously avoided by Mr. Pierpoint for he never touched it. The breach that ne left however, was eagerly stepped into by two of his doughty cham- pions, and it is with reference to-an illustiation by one of .these, viz.., Mr. Beck, that I now write. . .

Mr. Be'cit Blast rata 'his speech by supposing boots made in this country could be purchased at sty ss. per pair, while those made on the Continent could be pur- chased at say 4s. Gd. per pair. We buying the latter therefore considered that we were saving the difference in the prices. But Mr. Beck said this was an entire mistake, for instead of saving the differ- ence in the prices, the country lost the whole of the price charged for the foreign made article, which ho would prove. He then proceeded to say that half of the value of the boots as represented by the sale price of ss. was for labour costs, which would no longer be required and would therefore be lost, leaving 2s. od. to be accounted for. Of this, 2s. would go for the labour in making the leather, which would also be lost because for, still Il llongereav e arer to roe db,e or 4s. d. o far. the whole t)thoifs would would be absorbed by labour in the preparation of the accounted 6 s materials for tanning leather, but which would also be lost because no longer required. Thus, to show a debit and credit view of the transaction, the purchaser would stand to gain 6d., but the country would lose ss. I wish to state the case fairly and hope I have done so. Now the assumption that it rests upon is of the utmost improbability, and is impossible for the purposes of international trade. But the case is most superficially plausible, and if accepted without question, would deceive the unwary. It is, however, so flimsy a fabric, that a very little question- ing would destroy it utterly. In my previous letters I have shown that all international trade is simply ex- change of goods for goods. This is a truism acknow- ledged by all the Chancellors of the Exchequer of the last generation save one, and he merely a stop-gap, and by all the leading professors of political economy, not even excapting arr. Chamberlain's Prof( sear Ashley himself, and is so self-evident, that a very little im- partial thought results in a settled conviction. All goods, whether in the form of boots or anything else, coming to thiscountry, have to be paid for and though any single transaction may be settled by the sending of a cheque, the plural transactions between one country and another, are settled by the exchange of goods, so that if .Cl,OOO worth of boots or other goods are sent here, an equal value of goods or services will have to be given to pay for them. Therefore if £l,OOO worth of goods are received, the labour that would be ' employed in producing that particular worth of goods is rendered unnecessary, but the labour in producing the £l,OOO worth of , other goods to be given in exchange for them is not. This is what appears when we look a little below the surface, but on looking a little deeper, we shall see that more English labour is employed by this transaction than would be if it never occurred. For not only is the labour required for the production of the goods which are to be exchanged for the goods received, for those gods which come here wont do so of themselves, neither will the goods that are sent from this country, but they have to be carried, and generally I I speaking we are the carriers both ways. What then, of 'the immense number of dock labourers, sailors, railway' employes &c.? eiono f foreignlambaoduer articleeqaal results So far therefore as Mr. Beck's attempt to prove that athbesopluurtechadseesotrfutcht tithe selling price I price of that article is concerned, it is easy to see, that 1 under prevailing conditions, actually more labour may be required in producing the goods to exchange for them, and in carrying those goods to their destination. But there is another phase of this question, as given by Mr. Beck. First of all he tells us that one-half the selling price of the boots is repre- sented by labour, the other half by material. Secondly 4-sths of the selling price of the material that is required for the manufacture of the boots is represented by the labour required in the production of it, and 1-sth of tanning material. And, lastly, that the whole of the 1 selling price of the tanning material is represented by the labour employed in its production. Now here are three separate industries mentioned, Does Mr. Beck expect its to believe that the employer will require nothing in the shape of protit, or that the usual estab- lishment charges won't count, but that all this selling price of the foreign article is lost to the labour of this country ? I don't know whether 'qt. Back is new to the work of addressing audiences, but I will charitably assume that he is, and if so, he has a great deal yet to learn.— Yours faithfully, A. RATIIBONE,

The Mount, Grappenhall. January Iltb, 1905.

ENTHUSIASTIC MEETINGS LAST NIGHT. In Whltecross Ward. MR. CROSFIELD AND THE UNEMPLOYED QUESTION.

As the election draws nearer, enthusiasm in the Liberal ranks increise3. Another highly successful meeting in support of Mr. A. H. Crosfield's candidature was hold in St.. Barnabas' school, Bostock-street, on Thursday evening. The room was crowded and the greatest unanimity of feeling prevailed. Mr. Henry Roberts presided and was supported on the platform by Mr. A. U. Crostleld, Mr: and Mrs. C. B. Parker, Mrs. I Garnett, Mr. W. Madden, Mrs. Osborne. Mr. L. I Greening, Mr. S. R. Armitage, Mr. C. H. Hibbert, Mr. IR. M. Roberts, Mr. Nathan Cook, Mr. James Battersby I and others.

A CONSERVATIVE FREE TRADER,

Mr. L. GREENING, on behalf of the Free Trade League, said he resented the class of manufacturers who were not wailing about the country for Protection but who could fight their battles among the nations of the world with brains, up to date machinery, cheap food and raw material. (Cheers.) Marty of the manufac- tures who complained had antiquated machinery and old methods. Protectionists proposed to put the onus of taxation on the absolute necessities o f life and not on the luxuries of the rich. He would not be on the platform but for the reality of the tyranny of Protection. He knew of firms in this country, continued Mr. Greening who were buying raw material from Germany—which to the German was a finish article—at a cheap rate, and after completing it by skilled labour at the rate of 425. a week they sold it back to Germany at a profit. (Cheers.) He urged the andienca to vote solid for Mr. Croileld ann said he did:not believe the now Government would use the votes gained on this great issue to grant Home Rule. Before they began to deal witn that question they would appeal to the country. (Cheers.)

THE RESOLUTION. Mr. JAMES BATTERSISY, who appeared for the first time on a Liberal platform, moved a vote of confidence in Mr. Crosfield. Mr. An.mITAGE seconded. MR. CROSFIELD'S SPEECH.

Mr. CIVISFIELD, who was splendidly received, said that was the most stirring meeting he had had the privi- lege of addressing in that room. (Cheers.) They had still to fight for Free Trade, because attempts were still being made in this town, just as they were being made on the wider, national scale by the ex-Premier, deliber- ately to bamboozle the people. (A voice: "Throw- lug dust in their eyes.") Yes, it was nothing else. They were trotting out all their old horses—(laughter) —and the old arguments that were crushed by the great Sir Robert Peel sixty years ago. (Cheers.) It was a pity that at this time of day they who were genuine progressives and wished to spend their time and energy as they would presently in fighting for measures that would bring about more progress among the people, had to defer concentrating their efforts on that kind of work in order to beat back this dangerous, wicked, pernicious, and re-actionary!policy which the friends of a few privileged circles were trying to fasten on the whole nation. Alluding to

THE PROELENI OF THE UNENIPLOYED Mr. Crosfield said he had read what Mr. Morley had to say upon it the other day. If they could not abolish the , question of unemployment, which would take a very long time to do, he was certain they could do a great I deal to reduce it and he was also sure that they could not have a better 1111,11 to tackle that difficult and terrible question than the splendid working representa- tive who now sat in the Cabinet and who had not long since in the Albert gall a reception which marked ,a red letter day in the history of England and which did honour to the common-sense of ell the ranks in the progressive party of our country. (Cheers.) He agreed with what men like John Burns end Will Crooks said that it was a great mistake to simply chuck money about in charity. It was ' honest work that was wanted. (Cheers.) Further,they :agreed thet it was highly desirable and necessary that the work should be productive, remunerative, work that was to the advantage of the country. Afforestation, ' work on the fore shores, and other schemes of that kind were large undertakings that would give a great deal of employment, but he would point out that they did ' not want to make taxation heavier on the mass of the , people in order to deal with the unemployed. If they were going to make the rates too high they would just posh over the brink a large number of people who were not out of work but who found it difficult enough to make endshneet. If more capital were needed to give this kind of work a fair start, he said distinctly that he was sufficiently Radical to be quite prepared andanxious to see the time when people who, no doubt by great ability, energy alai foresight, but often also by a great deal of luck had become the groat plutocrats and the mighty, monied people of the country, should have to pay on a sensibly graduated scale of income tax, so that they might contribute more to the revenue of the country. (Cheers.) The question which he thought would do more than anything else except the preservation of Free Trade to raise the mass of the people who were on the lowest rung on the ladder, and which would bring in such a revenue that the question of the unemployed would not be deferred for lack of funds, was the great question of the whole taxation of land values. (Cheers.) More particularly Mr. Crosfield urged taxation of site values in the neighbourhood of crowded centres. Mr. Crosfield paid a warm tribute to British trade unionists, and expressed approval of the efforts they were making to bring the wages in Germany i nearer the higher level which obtained n this country. In this constituency, contianod the candidate, their opponents were circulating leaflets which contained arguments that were torn to rags and tatters sixty years ago in the House of Commons. We had, however, had two years of good discussion on the subject, and there was evidence at every meeting he addressed that men had thought the issue out for themselves, recognis- ing that it was not merely a game between the ins and ' outs but that it was a terrible thing to venture to uproot such a gigantic trade as we had in England. He had conchadeel Mr. Crosfield, a perfectly fair understanding with his Irish friends on the question , of

HONIE RULE lie felt convinced that in years to come, he hoped in I the not very distant future, the best solution of the question would be found in a representative body with an executive responsible to it, which would be able to put an end to the wretched, extravagant Government that was going on in Ireland at this moment, and to awaken a greater sense of responsibility in every Irish- man who had to think of the good of his country. He thought it only fair to say that while their friends of the Free Trade League differed with them on the ques- tion, it, would not be fair to take the votes they were helping to give them and the valuable patriotic and public spirited suppmt they were offering on this great Englishand Lancashire question of Free Trade, and use them to pass a measure of so-called Homo Rule, He used the word" so-balled " because perhaps the Tories as in the case of the land question, would give some sort of a bill, but they would not call it Home Rule and they would just say "A rose by any other name 'will smell as sweet." The coming election would be one of the most memorable battles in political history and he trusted Warrington would have a share in the victory—a victory which in future time the pen of history would delight to trace (Loud cheers).

IN ST. PETER'S SCHOOL. Crowded Gathering.

COUNCILLOR MINN & MR W. P. RYLANDS.

Scenes of the utmost enthusiasm characterized a gathering which was held in S. Peter's schoolroom on Thursday evening in support of the candidature of Mr. Crostield. The room was crowded to an uncomfortable extent and the speakers and the candidate were cheered to the echo. Mr. W. Furness presided, and was sup- ported amongst others by : Messrs. S. R. Armitage, B. ' J. Guest, J. Howes, L. Greening, C. Jackson Holmes and Councillor Magian. Mr. FURNESS briefly contrasted the conditions in Ger- many with the conditions under which the workingmen of England worked, pointing out that the German worked fromltito 10 hoursimore per week than the British workman and received less wages. lie pointed out how a large number of meat shops in Germany had had to be closed as the prices where so higb as to prohibit the workingman from purchasing beef. Mr. S. It. AgmErAon, who represented the local Branch of the Free Trade League, especially instanced the cotton trade as an industry which had benefited and was still benefltting as tt,e result of our free trade policy.

===== 0002601_19060113_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
SPEECH BY MR. J. HOWES.

Mr. HOWES, who had a hearty reception, said that last century we had experienced Protection. If tariff! were good for trade those must have been the days &- England's greatest prosperity, but we found that then ' food was dear and wages were low. Since then we had adopted Free Trade or if the Tories liked, free imports and food had come down in price and wages had gone up. (Cheers.) To-day we were the greatest trading nation in the world; we had a greater foreign trade than any nation with a like population ; we were the greatest carriers in the world the greatest ship builders. Workmen in England got higher wages than they did in any protected country except the United States, and there the cost of living was so high that the purchasing power of wages was reduced. (Cheers). He would like to refer to the iron trade. \Ve in England had iron in abundance and yet could not pro- deceit as cheaply as the German. What was the cause? It was the English landlords. (Cheers.) The Tories recommended as a remedy for German competition in the iron trade the putting on of a tax to stop German iron corning into England. What was wanted was that the tax should be taken off at home. (Cheers.) England was the only country in the world where the landlord owned the minerals, which in other countries , belonged to tbe State. For every ton of pig iron pro- duced in England the landlord received a tax of Gs. Gd. With that tax, removed we could compete successfully with the whole of the world. (Cheers.) Did they wonder that British industries suffered? He wondered that they existed at all and they only existed because Free Trade was elastic enough to enable us to trade with the whole of the world despite tariff walls. According to the Tories we had to stop foreign goods coming into ' England. For every cargo which came into this country a cargo went out in payment and if none came in none would go out and we would strike a blow at shipping. When a •blow was struck at the shipping we would be the ones to suffer to the greatest extent and that suffering would result in unemployment, increased competition in the labour market, and consequently in the lowering of wages. (Cheers.) If the Tories were ' reformers as they posed as being, they should not put ' tariffs on but should take royalties off. Instead of in- creasing the burden they should remove the burden which already pressed heavily upon the shoulders of the people. (Cheers). Councillor 111,toixx referred to a speech made by Mr. Peter Rylands at a recent Conservative meeting. Mr. Peter Rylands' uncle, Mr. John Rylands once said that for years past they had paid dividends by importing German wire and re-exporting it. If there WAS a man there who worked for Mr. Rylauds be asked him " Does not German wire come into that works to-day ?" (A voice : Correct.") Those were the

TARIFF REFORMERS. Those were the men who had made their fortunes under Free Trade and who when they were rich enough to be independent ofj the workingmen wanted other means to make them richer still whilst tho poor would be made poorer. (Cheers.) Mr. E. J. GuEsr said they had no need to he afraid of the Home Rule bogey." If Home Rule did come it would not come in the next Parliament. They had the assurance of the Prime Minister and others on that point. (Applause.) Moreover Home Rule would never pun the House of Lords. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Guest con- cluded with a strong appeal to the electors to support Mr. .Crostiold at the polling, an appeal which was received with cheers, At the request of a member of the audience Coun- cillor Maginn spoke briefly on the question of Home Rule.

Mr. Cdosrmo, who was received with prolonged cheering, said that, with reference to Free Trade every effort which was made 60 years ago to delude the great mass of the people in the interests of the few, every effort to mystify the people which was then made, was being tried over again. In high places we found the ex-Premier not only mystifying everyone in the land, but, he firmly believed, mystifying himself. (Hear, I hear.) The smaller fry were taking their cue from that landdrying to win the battle on side issues, and like Mr. ; Balfour were he was going to say, attempting to square the circle. (Applause.) As they were trying to square the circle of the Free Trade question they were trying to throw a little dust into the eyes of the people with regard to Chinese labour. The Liberals appealed Ito the electors chiefly on the two great issues of Free Trade and Chinese labour but there were other great questions before the electors. The Liberal party were determined that they would not be satisfied with the condition of the population as it existed in our most crowded centres and sadder still in the slums of our great cities. Fresh sources of taxation must be opened out and they were entitled to tax some of the untapped sources—(hear, hear)—and the man whose income was £lOO,OOO or or £200,000 a year was well able to bear the weight of a graduated income tax which would prove a sconce° of great revenue to the country.(Applause)With reference to Chinese labour Mr. Crosfleld said the Liberals were asked why they did not send,all the Chinese back at once. They however could not replace 45,000 or 50,000 men in a " jiffy "and the evil of the thing lay in the Tories having introduced it. The Liberal party , were going to stop the further importation of Chinese and were going to see that they were re-patrioted when the terms of their indentures were complete. (Cheers). The Tories had been drawing comparisons between the British Guiana and the South African labour ordnances. The Coolies in the West Indies could settle in the country and conld hold property whereas the Coolies in South Africa remained aliens all the time they were in that colony, they were shut out from the rest of the country and were treated as out- casts. Could any one say that such a thing as I that WWI good for that country. (" No "and " Shame.") He had been called a little Englander and he called that a piece of impudence. He' had as much pride in the Empire as anyone. He had beer} in Canada and rejoiced at the devotion there expressed to the mother country, and he believed it was much better to bind the Empire together by principles than to attempt to do so by introducing into the Imperial household little sordid questions of batgain likely to give rise to jealousy and differences in the future. (Cheers.) The reading of the manifesto of the Warrington Women's Liberal Association by Missj Crosfield was greeted with applause. On the motion of Mr. H. GUEST, seconded by FELIX Maoixs, a resolution endorsing the candidature of Mr. Crosffeld was heartily carried. _ --

The fiscal issue was a great issue but there were other reforms which must come. Extended registration must come and the Liberal party would take up that question and see, as was only right, that when the tax collector called at their houses the overseer would put their names on the list of voters. The workers would not be ungrateful and could always be trusted to be actuated by no mean spirit of revenge. When they got that power they would remember the men who had given it to them and would work side by side with them in the common cause of humanity. (Cheers.) THE CHINESE LABOUR QUESTION.

Tory Misrepresentation.

In view of the taunt reiterated by Mr. Balfour and , emphasised by Mr. R. Pierpoint in Warrington, that as coolie labour was established or permitted in the West Indies by the Liberals the action of the present Govern- ment in regard to Chinese labour in the Transvaal can only be prompted by party exigencies, the "Daily Chronicle "has received an interesting communication trom the \Vest India Committee, signed "N. Lubbock, chairman," and" Algernon E. Aspi nail, secretary." The system of importation of Chinamen into South Africa (says the writers) differs very materially and in many important respects from that which obtains in British Guiana. The following points drawn from the respective ordinances and regulations at present in force show this :

In South Africa the Chinese immigrant (a) must not be employed in skilled labour and certain trades so long as he remains in the colony. In British Guiana the East Indian immigrant (a) if he shows aptitude can be employed as tradesman and mechanic, or in other skilled work in the factory during his indenture. There is no restriction whatever after his indenture ceases. During his indenture he frequently saves money and purchases cattle, which are looked after for him during his work at the expense of the estate. In South Africa the Chfnese immigrant (b) must be sent back to China at the termination of his indenture. In British Guiana the Ea...t. Indian immigrant (b) has the option of remaining in the colony, lie can receive Crown lands instead of his half return passage ; he is encouraged to remain on the estate, where he receives free housing, medical attendance, and pasture for his. cattle, of which and other privileges a large number avail themselves.

Tn South Africa the Chinese immigrant (c) must not leave the premises where he is employed without a per- mit. In British Guiana the East Indian immigrant (c) must not leave the estate without a permit, which can easily be obtained; but as the estates vary from 1,000 to 10,000 acres in size and are communities in themselves this is no hardship. In South Africa the Chinese immigrant (d) summary juris- diction is exercised by the superintendent and inspector In British Guiana, regarding the East Indian immigrant (d)proceedings for breach of contract can only be taken before the ordinary magisrates of the colony. The immigration authorities only inquire and report to the governor on grievances. In South Africa the Chinese immigrant (e) may be accompanied by his vife or not at option. In British Guiana the East Indian immi- grants (3) must be accompanied by 40 per cent, of women.

GREAT MEETING IN THE PARR HALL. --- Mr. Crosfield and the Foreign Labour Cry.

One of the largest and most enthusiastic political meetings ever held in Warrington took place in the Parr Hall on Friday evening in support of the candidature of Mr. A. H. Crosfleld. Long before the time announced for the commencement of the proceedings, the hall wan crowded in every pert, and as the stream of people anxious to obtain admission continued, the doors Ind to be closed and an overflow meeting was arranged for Cairo-street schools. Throughout the greatest enthusiasm prevailed, and the candidate htd a magnifi- cent reception. Mr. C. E. PArker presided, and was supported on the platform by many prominent Liberal workers.

The CILIARY AN in the course of an interesting address said he considered the battle of Free Trade had already been fought and won. (Cl:were.) There was every reason to suppose that the general election would con- firm the verdict of the by-eleetions, and that Warrington would do her pert. Personally. he had never thought the crude proposals of Mr. Chamberlain would carry the country. Englishmen bad too much brain to be taken in by sneh ill-adjusted proposals. The question was too simple to demand laboured thousht. Contrast that problem with the infinitely more dialcnit question of national education. Some of there he 4 givee that much thought. seeking for a solution and he did not think they yet saw clear daylight through it. He wished the difficulty could be lifted out of the sphere of party politics. For the children's Rake we ought to approach this question with a broad, Christian charity, and while every shade of thought should be true to its principles they ought to strive to look at the question all round and especially try to see what was true and good in the outlook of those who differed from them. At any rate those who were representatives; of different Christian Churches should say to each other, " Come, and let us reason together, and see whether it is not possible to arrange some plan which will best conduce to the phrsical, intellectual, and moral well-being of the children." (Cheers.) Mr. Ceosereto was received with loud cheers and musical honours. After thanking the audience for their stirring reception he said he had hoped they might have fought this election as they fought the last election on the big national questions, and that irrelevant and piti- ful personalities—(" Shame ")—might have been left out. (Cheers.) He confessed it was with astonish- ment he saw certain charges brought forward because it was not the first time that this little game had boon tried. It was now half a dozen years since the same question was brought forward with regard to the firm with which he had the honour to be connected. (Cheers.) At. that. time he showed in the Pres, that the proportion of foreigners and of foreign intellect at their concern at Bank Quay was absolutely minute. He really thought that the matter had boon dealt with for ever. But no ;it had been brought up again, but by the action of those two colleagues of his, one of whom was on the platform—(cheers)—this absolutely paltry attempt had been knocked on the head. He (Mr. Crosfleld)had some share in the original introdection of that minute fraction of foreign brain which had been so useful—(bear, hear)—in many departments of their great concern, and which had helped very much to pro- vide an enormous increase of labour which was going on - (cheers)—and he was happy to say would go on for many a year to come. So their friends found they were on the wrong tack, that it was a sort of mare's nest. Now they were going on a third tack, and they thought that because he was interested to a certain extent, a substantial extent, in the development of a new, and a clever and valuable patent, and because part of the manufacture of that patent Was in a foreign country, that they would have him on the hip there. He (the speaker) was interested in the patent, and, as it happened, so were a couple of the finest commercial men in England. whose names they would respect if it was worth his while to quote them. He supposed his friends thought they had a good case, but they had a jolly bad one. The article they manu- factured, which was a patent wax match, was made to some extent in a foreign country, and it illustrated most beautifully the extent to which Warrington de- pended on partly manufactured articles that (tame in as raw material for their factories and labour. In the first place 2ii percent. of what they were manufacturing was wholly manufactured in England, and what about the remaining 75 per cent. ? A great part of the labour was being done in England because the matches were shipped over here in hulk, all the boxes in which they packed them and all the labels were manufactured in England, and the laboav they employed in England was already considerable, In a few months they were going to transport all that 75 per cent, over to England. (Cheers). He did not say the factory in Sweden would be left unoccupied, but it would be occupied like other factories abroad by a cheaper clam of labour, and a manufacture that was leas suited to this country. As to employing poor Swedes in Gothenberg at that kind of work, if anybody was going to take exception to that they might as well go to his friend, Mr, Lever, who was one of the most progressive of employers and say, Oh yes, you have a line big factory at Port Sunlight, but what about manufacturing for foreign countries af-wond ?" where Mr. Lever had soapworks. 'This was indeed a pitiful attempt. He was glad he did not know who were the responsible gentlemen on the Conservative side who countenanced and encouraged that kind of thing, and, what was more, he did not want to know. (Cheers.) While the Liberal cause would not suffer in the slighteet degree by those wretched attempts to distract the public mind from the great questions they had got to settle, he would tell his Tory friends that he hoped they would not go much further for their own sakes—(epplanse)—becnuse if they did he was afraid they would find there world be a lasting stain of ignominy upon polities of 'that kind. (Cheers.) In times gone by Warrington had had a somewhat unenvi- able reputation in regard to the way in which election contests had been fought. end he strongly appealed to all right minded citizens, whatever be their coloar,creed class, profession, or business, to end that record. He heard only a day or so ago that it was nett(' be a thing of the past. The Liberals of Warrington were fighting this battle under the leadership of the Chairman, who had not only put energy into the party, but taught them to look high to fine ideals,and to fight their battle with absolutely clean hands. (Cheers.) Perhaps a good many in that hall might know that there were not a few of the licensed victuallers of Warrington with whom he had a fairly intimate relationship, and some of them he might call personal friends, including their honoured President, Alderman Evans—(applanse)—and he was sure they would be the last that would lend themselves to the practices he had referred to. He knew a great many of them intended to see that there was fair play and he belieyed the Liberals could appeal with success in their endeavour not only during this election but for all time to put an end to the practices to which he had alluded. He did not think there had been any excuse for the introduction of those personalities that had so sadly missed fire; but, of course, one could understand—whether they looked to their record of the past or to the really fine programm• of a really fine Ministry that had been put before them —the outlook was not over cheerful for those fighting against them. (Applause.) Mr. Crosfleld alluded to the great causes which Liberals had to fight for, and in conclusion appealed to his hearers to help to swell the great tide of opinion on which depended the prosperous voyaging of humanity. (Cheers). A resolution in support of Mr. Crosaeld's candidature, was proposed by Alderman Ptertrotser, seconded by Mr. MADDEN, and carried with only one dissentient. A vote of thanks was accorded the speakers a chairman, on the proposition of Alderman TlN:sind seconded by Councillor EVAN!.

TIE OVERFLOW MEETING.

Mr. Crosfield on Capital and Labour.

There was a crowded attendance at the overflow meet- ing held in Ceiro-street schools and Mr. Crosfield had another great reception.—The Rev. C. Harvey Couk presided. In the course of his address Mr. Crostiold urged pro- gressives to work for higher ideals in government and with that end in view, to do their utmost to further I education. Continuing ha said they believed there was lan enormous gain to humanity in &island to be pro- cured, but only when they had a progressive majority strone enough to show that the opinion of the nation would not stand any more trilling, any more eineseala- tion of their work by the House or Lords. (Cheers). They hoped and believed that the strength and the manhood of the nation would insist on carrying the measures which would do so much for the people. They knew very well what those measures were. First of all, partly 119 a source of revenua—revenue that might be applied not to plunder and blunder all over the world but to the amelioration of the whole condition of the people—and not less in order that they might again revive more of the country life that was wanted in this land, the question of the taxation of lend values and site values was one of the greatest reforms they could ever work for. (Cheers.) He had felt that very deeply, and he did not know anything that would raise the conditions in the humble homes and cottages of this country more;than that great reforrn.Then they knew that Liberals had as their standard and their aim in life the preservation of a real and genuine civilimtion amongst workers of the land. They did not aim at entire equality—nature was against that—and he for one had always tried to work for what he honestly beliered in. He could assure them that he would never strive and work for anything else. (Cheers.) When he spoke strongly on the land question it was because be felt it. He was also strongly in favour of equality before the law for employer and employed, of abolishing the judge-made law which had put in jeopardy Solle of the greatest organizations for workers that we had ever seen in the world—the great British trade anions. (Cheers.) In regard to trade unions, *host which so much Was Ftaid,anybody who had looked into history ought to know that those who worked in favour of and believed in defending the right of collective bargains were aot making for strife. He hated industrial strife only less than he hated 'bloody wars. No they were working for reason in place of those passions of wild animals which unfortnnetely,concealed under the thin veneer of civili- zation were ready to be awakened and let loose—some- times by irresponsible statesmen who were not ashamed to appeal to what they never ought to dare to appeal to (Cheers). No, they did not want to appeal to those passions. He never would do it, and certainly not in our industrial concerns, because, after all, what was it bu civil war, when they saw one of those frightful conflicts in England? They had all, he was sure, thought a great deal about the solution of those ques- tions. He would not suggest that whet suited one country immediately seited another. They had indi- viduality in nations as they had amongst individuals. They knew, of course, that at the last great Congress of Labour compulsory arbitration was refused. He did not think we wanted it either. He did not know whether we should iu future, but he Was sure it would be a mis- take in England yet. We were getting a greater force of public opinion every day and oommon-sense was going to tell. Conciliation Boards were also going to tell—(hser, hear)—hecanse if they ; got a strong Conciliation Board and a strong force of public opinion, the employer or employee was going to incur a most serious and dangerous responsibility if he refused to listen to the voice of ransom and simply said "I will use brute force " or take an unfair advantage on any given situa- tion, Mr. Crosfield conclnded by el:pressing his gratifi- cation that there had been such au awakening of public spirit in Warrington, and said Liberals were fighting for one of the mightiest causes that had ever been fought for in the whole of British history. (Cheers.) Alderman PreetroiNT and Mr. HONTXS also addressed the meeting.

IN ST. JOHN'S WARD.

Crowded and Enthusiastic Meetings.

SPEECHES BY MR. A. H. CROSFIELD, MR W. MADDEN AND OTHERS.

Free Trade League Pronouncement.

'Councillor Maginn and the Home Rule " Bogey."

IThe Liberal Candidate on Monday evening addressed his constituents iu St. John's Ward. A meeting had , been arranged to be held in the Briok-street Mission ; hut long before the speech-making commenced the ; building was packed and an overflow meeting had to be : held in the Friars' Green Mission. Councillor Maginn I presided over the gathering in Brick-street Mission and i was supported by Mr. A. H. Crosfleld,Mr. C. E. Parker, 1 Rev. C. Harvey Cook, Mr. William Madden, Mr. C. Jackson Holmes, Mr. Owen Toole, Mr. Sexton, and ; delegates from the Warrington Branch of the Free , Trade League. I COUNCILLOR MAGINN AND HOME RULE.

Conncillor MAGI, who had a hearty reception, said the Tory party wanted to shift the issue to florae Rule, and were depending upon the prejudice and bigotry which existed in England 25 years ago. (Hear, hear.) Home Rule was fronght with as much interest to Englishmen as to Irishmen. They would all be greatly surprised if the teeming population of Lancashire was to emigrate until the county was left a desolate vrsste and would naturally say that there was something : radically wrong. (Haar, hear.) When Ireland was per- an aded to give the management of her affairs to Eng:and Ireland was not declining in the least. Ihe country once supported a population of 8.000.000 and was prosperous. To-day Ireland was only sole to support 4,500,000 and could only keep that number in misery, poverty, and unhappiness. What was wrong that such a change had been brought about? The airs rivers, valleys, and mountains were the same, and thd same race occupied the soil. What was wrong was ha laws. He did not mean that the laws which were made in the British House of Commons were bad, bet it was the administration of those laws which was wrong. (Cheers). The brain, blood, and sinew of Ireland was being driven oat of that country, and there was not a single Irish boy or girl but felt that they were pushed out of their happy homes by England, and learnt to hate England. (Cheers). Irishmen wanted to change all that, and wanted to brine about

===== 0002601_19060113_art0143.txt | included=true | included =====
A UNION OF

instead of a union of laws. (Cheers). Councillor Maginn clearly illustrated how the constant emigration of the Irish led to a snrpina of labour in the English market and consequently to unemployment and lessened wages. He pointed oat how Lord Duoraven, formerly a great opponent of Home Rile, after having had experience in the management of Ireland, had changed his opinion until he fought hard for the freedom of the Irish people. (Cheers.) Irishmen did not for one moment desire a separation from England. (Cheers). They were as proud of the British Empire as any Englishman alive, and any crazy sohetne to set Ireland alone in the midst of the great nations of Europe would he fought against by every sensible Irishman including himself. (Cheers.) They asked what was given to every colony, the right to manage their own affairs, end he appeuled to them to consider the interest which Irishmen had in England and to think was it likely, in face of that interest, that they would do any- thing to bring aboat a separation from England? Cheers.) The manifesto issued by the Warrington Women's Liberal Association was then read by Mrs. Furness.

ENTHUSIASTIC MEETING AT ORFORD.

England's Unexampled Prosperity

AS SHOWN BY BOARD OF TRADE

RETURNS.

Not since the memorable election when Mr. J. G. McMinuies was returned has a Liberal meeting been held in the village of Orford. It is regarded as ahappy augury for th• SUCCS,B of the Free Trade and procres• sive gauge that the meeting held in the schools on Tues- day evening in support of Mr. A. H. Crosneld was splendidly attended. The room was crowded and enthusiasm ran high. Councillor J. Wilkinson presided and was supported on the platform by the candidate, Hiss Crosfield, Mr. C. E. Parker, and Mr. S. Rigby Armitage.

The CHAIRMAN reminded the ineetiny, that during the ten years the Tories had been in oblice taxes and national expenditure had increased,and the national debt had gone up by leaps and bounds. The purchasing power of the workingman's /wee had been diminished. Ile was sure they would all agree with him that it was high time they had a change of Government and if they , returned Mr. Crosfield they would have in hima worthy representative of the Borough. (Cheers). ' Mr. C. E. PARK RR delivered an interesting address which was listened to with great attention. He pointed out that the election would not be fought out alto- gether on the clear division between Conserva- tism and Liberalism. The important question of Protection and Free rrade was the great issue before the country. Three years ago both political parties were prepared to maintain the policy which had been in operation in the country for fifty years, but Mr. Chamberlain now proposed to turn away from all his previous opinions, and wanted the country to revert to a form of Protection. Ile gate as his reason that the prosperity of the country had reached its climax. Mr. Parker showed how since Ur. Chamber- lain began his propaganda the country had prospered more than ever. According, to the Board of Trade returns, the increase of exports during the past year had beea3d millions, and the increase in manufactured goods alone was 22 millions. The past yearivres one of unexampled prosperity as far as exports went. (Cheers.) Mr. Parser emphasised the point that the effect of Mr. Chamberlain's policy would undoubtedly be to raise the prices of the articles which were protected. He also showed how inter-dependent many industries were, the consequence being that Protection of one industry would increase the cost of carrying Oh another. Whether he was a Liberal or not, at this time when the great Fres Trade issue was raised he would say most emphati- cally that, whatever might be his political views in other respects, he would vote for the Free Trade candidate. (Cheers.) Mr. A. 11. CI:9SFI LLD, who had a most cordial recep- tion, said Mr. Parker had referred to the inter- dependence of industries. lie (Mr. Crosfleld) frankly confessed that it astonished him to hear employers of labour advocating Prot • ction, who seemed to be blind to the disasters that would follow to their owe businesses, and therefore to themselves and their employees, if Protection were adopted. Alluding to the tanning industry and its far-reaching operations, Mr. Crosfield asked what would be the effect on many of the tanneries in Warrington if to any serious extent the mightiest industry or Lancashire, and with ono exception the mightiest insiustry of all England, were to suffer by our adopting the rash, ill-considered, and positively foolish proposals that were put before us. They knew perfectly well what a big market there WAS in Lancashire among the cotton spinners for Warring- ton leather, and there was no industry, not excepting ship building itself, that would be more severely hit by Protection than cotton in Lancashire. It had been fre- quently stated that about four million souls in Lanca- shire were interested in

Trta CoTTON TADr and a further calculation had hoes made that if they included those who were indirectly concerned in the prosperity of British cotton, the number was not less than ten millions,or one fourth of our whole population. The figures regarding the output of cotton were always worth remembering and considering. The raw cotton that we imported was 40 millions sterling in vales and out of that we manufactured and exported 70 millions sterling. using 20 milliens for ourselves. That repre- sented attotal manufacture of the value of 90 millions of different fabrics of cotton. (Cheers.) Continuing Mr. Croseadd said he thought that when they considered the figures quoted by Mr. Parker which iovi denced our great national prosperity, they would agree that the old ' saying that "a bird is the band is worth two in the bush " was particularly applicable in this case. (Hear hear.) Fortunately in this issue they could appeal to reason. They were not appealing to people during some time of war when passion ram very high. They could appeal to lesson and they could quote the enormous trade that Britain did in the great neutral markets of the world. He would venture to remind them of tho fact that those markets were in- creasing in consumption and that as education began to make its way that consumption would be vastly greater. (Cbeera.) Mr. Croofield instanced China and India as countries which offered splendid markets as erlueation progressed, hnd expressed hie approval of the policy of the " open-door " in China which must tend to the development of that great country. (Cheers.) He also emphasised the fact that by thecheapuess of production which our Free Trade policy ensured, England could compete with advantage against Protected conntries in the various Protected markets of the world. When they considered the facts they would say at ()Roe: " Britain must have the great pro- dominant.. We mast be the great importers and

CORIIEPONDENTS are particularly requested to send letters intended for Friday's " Examiner " not later than Thursday morning.

Councillor Craik and the Election.

Sir,—Kindly permit me, through the medium of your Saturday's issue, to say, that my name, which has nppeared on political posters in the town this week, has been publishel entirely without my mms.nt,aid JAS. J. CRAIX. Green flank, kfanchester-road, Warrington. January 11th, 190 C.

Tory Friendship.

Sir, —Tory ‘,Priendellip : Mast is it ? This Word says " Ye shall know them by their fruits." It appears that even the " Tory party " now-a-days is paying special attention to the " working class." Why ? The reply is not far-fetched : Simply because it is election time. Once this affair will be a thing of the past,. Con- servatism—As ever—without the slightest hesitation, will entirely ignore the working class as not worthy of env_consirleration.

If Toryism is:such a friend of the working people as in these days it pretends to be, will Mr. Pierpoint, as the Tory candidate, tell the workingmen of Warriweton how he voted in the House of Commons when the 'case of the Penrhyn Quarries dispete came on ? Did Mr. Pierpoint and the Conservative party prove themselves then friends of the working people ? Tf a Conservative Government could not be a friend of Bethesda working men, it cannot be a friend of the Warrington working men, neither. NViis Mr. Pierpoint the workman's friend in the Bethesda dispute ? Certainly not. Why not ? Simply becatiee Toryism has ever been, and will ever be, the bitterest enemy of the wPrking people.i And I trust that the workingmen of Warrington will n the near future prove themselves far in advance oven of the local i Rector, who betrays so much ignorance n taking upon himself to inform them what they should do.—Yours itc., W. J. ROBERTS. Parker-street.

Mr. Pierpoint and Chinese Labour.

Sir,—The Conservative Candidate's address does not contain a reference to Chinese labour and yet it is a " burning " question of the hour. Of course Mr. , Pier-point voted for the introduction of this malicious system and the least said the better from his point of view. What I would like to know is : If the Tory Government thought it right to prohibit Europeans landing in England whose only crime is that they are poor in a number of instances, why is it also right to import into a British colony, won by British soldiers and paid for by British money. 50,000 Chinese coolies, the scum of theit country ? There are two gentlemen contesting Eng,lish constituencies now who fought through the war and afterwards governed portioua of the conquered provinces who emphatically declare that Chinese labour is unnecessary and moreover not favoured by the South African general public. Mr. Pierpoint mentions colonial preference in his address, but there is an object lesson in " sugar " on that point. In 1904-5 we paid practically as much for 1,373.000 tons of sugar as we did for 1,711.000 tons in, 1900-1. Mr. Foster Clarke, chief of the firm of Foster Clarke and Co.. Eiffel Tower Works, Maidstone, a firm employing more than 600 hands, says :—Their firm has had to pay £14,850 in sugar dnty.lf this .f.:14,850 had goa- into the business instead of paying out as a tax their firm would have been able to employ a hundred addi- tional hands. Is it not absurd in face of these facts to I say the foreigner pays the tax ? Retaliation or Pro- tection must seriously hamper trade in this country. Yours, &c., If. F.

CHRISTMAS.

Summer with its joys has passed, Christmas is approaching last; • The days fly on as in a dream, Peace an happiness reign supreme. Christmas morning, the postman hails, Letters —what excitement prevails— With what eager looks he is faced, Giving packets in breathless haste. , Then smiling faces what need tell the rest, The thoughts of joy these emblems suggest. A new joy, presents, where they are given, More smiling faces where pleasure is riven. Although it is a happy time Many children in every clime Do not know a single joy, Not in the form of a little toy. We should be very thankful, indeed, NVlio have plenty, to do some kind deed, Give just a toy from which you can part And it will gladden some poor heart. - HAROLD SHARPE (age 14). The Catholic Vote.

Sir,—At this juncture none but the h tlf-witted can be so woefuliy ignorant as not to 'lava made up their minds on the.subjects at issue. The English Catholics have openly taken sides and we must follow suit. We are not quite so much interested in English politics as they, but the welfare of our own land has still a warm. corner in our hearts. The last swing of the pendulum gave us twenty years of firm governmo t, for which we are suitably grateful. The next swing may not be of the same duration, but in Trish affairs we are promised government a little more after our own Irish ideal. Though lunacy and depopulation have inc, eased, Mr. Pierpoint might very well give those of us left credit for more commonsense that to hold forth in such ponderous terms that by Home Rule we mean the disintegration of the Empire. We are not as yet as blind nor as stupid. The success of Our local govern- nient is a matter of pride and both English parties have noticed the change for the better. In-as-much as the Liberals have pledged themselves to give us a further similar change which the Tories will as strenuously .deny to us, our duty on Monday is plain. Our duty as Irishmen is to remember Ireland and never has it been made so clear to us. One does not care to anticipate but it is not too im- probable that the Tory party in time and with a desire to cement the Empire together may see its way to change its tactics, and actually give us better treatment—per- haps Home Role under another name. Strange somer- saults have been recorded in politics and our duty then would be just as clear as it is now. Until the Tory party condescends to see Trish affairs through Irish glasses we cannot look at English affairs through Tory glasses.—Yours &c, „

" DOOLEY."

A NEW SOAP.—" of soaps the there aro no end,' once said Dickens. If it was forty years ago, how much more must it be true to-day, and the difficulty of obtaining or selecting a good soap has vastly increased since the days of Dickens. A new soap has been placed on the market by the British Empire Soap Co., which the proprietor claim will " cleanse the world "—this is a large order bet the cleansing qualities of the soap seem to justify all that is claimed for it. There is a competition in connection with the use of the soap which, in addition to the advantages accruing to the user may be of substantial cash value. For particulars see our advt. columns.

CARE OF THE SICK POOR.

Proposed Resident Medical Officer at

the Workhouse.

DR. MAIMS COMPENSATION.

The proposed appointment of a resident medical officer was under consideration at the meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians on Wednesday. Mr. J. Billing° presided anti there were also present :—Mrs. Clay, Mrs. Wilkinson, Miss Broadbent, Rev. Dr. Mercer, Rev. T. R. Matthews, Dr. Murray, Dr. Patterson,M easrs. W. Hickman, A. J. Jolley, R. feorshaw, T. Ritchie, S. Davies, J. Payton, R. Bolton, A. Brooks, G. Thompson, M. W. Seton, R. Flannagan; G. Mather R. Bacl, house, W. Houghton, J. Cook, J. C. Sutton (clerk), S. Sutton (assistant clerk), J. F. Awbery (t-aster), and A. Wright (superintendent of Padgate schools). Mr. FORSHAW moved a resolution thas in the event of a resident medical officer being appointed, Dr.Braide the present visiting medical officer,should be paid £4O a year as compensation for the loss of his office. 'He regretted that he was unable to go further at that meet- ing and move that the necessary steps be taken for the appointment of a resident medical officer, but he under- stood that the matter of compensation had to he decided first. He felt very strongly that they ought to have a resident medical officer both on the grounds of economy and the humane treatment of the inmates. He did not think they could secure full efficiency under the exist- ing arrangements. Mr. JOLLEY, in seconding, went very fully into the financial side of the question. He pointed out that an important point was the training of probationer nurses. Through changes of legislation the certificate which the Board now gave the probationers was practically worthless. If nothing was done by the Board to improve the training and status, the holding mit of inducements to girls to become probationers would amount to trying to secure them by false pretences. If they had a resi- dent medical officer the hospital wouldbe recognized as a training school, and the certificate the Board could give at the end of three veers would possess a definite and permanent value. 0-n the other hand, if the resi- dent medical officer were not appointed the Board would have to appoint tra:ned nurses, and incur an additional annual expenditure of £llO. About 1,200 cases passed through the hosptial each year and they were in hospital, on an average, for eight weeks. The majority of the members of the House Committee were agreed that if Dr. Braide's services were retained it would be necessary to prescribe that he must attend at the hospital at least two hours daily and some of them thought the work could not be properly done in less than three hours. If they took that course they would have to increase Dr. Braide's salary. From correspondence with the Local Governmeut Board, it was perfectly clear that Dr. Braide's position could not be disturbed except as the result of amicable negotia- tion. The new arrangement, Mr. Jolley contended, would decrease the annual expenditure by ..C3O. If they retained the present system and put their hospital administration on a proper footing they would have an increase in the annual expenditure of at least 4:100. If they appointed an assistant resident medical officer and a visiting medical officer the expenditure would work out very much as it was now. Mr. TiroatesoN moved an adjournment of the dis- cussion for a fortnight. Mr. S. DAVIES seconded but The CHAIRMAN declined to accept the proposition as the meeting was called specially to deal with the question. Nfr. HICKMAN said he was now convinced that the appointment of a resident medical officer was the right step to take. Sooner or later the Board would have to adopt that course end he thought it was a great mistake that the Board did not adopt the scheme when the new Infirmary was opened. If they had done so there would have been no necessity to talk about compensation. The arguments which had been placed before the meeting were, in his opinion, incontrovertible and be could not see bow anyone could vote against the proposal. Miss linoanersr said she had felt strongly on the matter ever since she first began to take an interest in the Workhouse. She did not wish to imply that Dr. Braide did not do all that was possible in the time he spent at the Workhouse, but she thought the time was not sufficient. She considered also that they were doing a most cruel wrong to the nurses by not having proper arrangements for their training. Mr. FLatss:aoas: said he objected to the 440 com- pensation being paid. Dr. Braide, he believed, came there under an agreement to be terminated at six months notice, and there was no mention then of com- pensation. He also questioned whether it would be necessary for them to have trained nurses if a resident medical officer were not appointed. Mr. MATHER. said the three interests to consider were those of the 'sick, the nurses and the ratepayers. He did not think the Board should entrust their sick poor to the care of a young student whom they would get at the salary they would offer. He thought that would also have an effect on the nursing.lf they wished to obtain the services of an older medical man in whom they might have full confidence they would have to offer a much higher sum. DT. PATTERSON supported the proposed appointment on the ground that it would increase the effiiciency. of the Infirmary and enable the nurses to thoroughly qualify for places as trained nurses. He thought, how- ever, that in addition to the resident medical officer they would also require a visiting medical officer. The amount they would otherwise have to pay for trained nurses would alinost be equal to the salary of a resi- dent doctor.

Bev. Dr. MERCER thought the question might be ad- journed for a month in order that the members might consider the matter more fully. Mr. Jolley had said that unless they paid a resident medical officer they would have to appoint trained nurses. Ile did not admit that, as the Warrington Union was at present in the position of the vast majority of other Unions. The °Hoard had not simply to consider the sick indoor poor. Why should the man outside the Workhouse who did his very best to keep outside be penalised to keep others who had not in their hey-day made provision lca theinselves,in a better way than himself? After further discussion, Mr. FORSHAW said he thought the Committee had made a very good bargain with [Jr. Braide, and ought to have the thanks of the Board for it. As to the resident medical officer, the Board could depend upon it that they intended to got an Mlicient man. They did not intend employing two doctors if they could possibly help it. The resolution was carried by 11 votes to B. It was reported that at a meeting of the House Com- mittee, the deputation appointed to visit the Burnley and Prescot Unions with reference to the proposedrcsi- dent medical officer, presented a report, but no decision was arrived at.

DONATION.

On the motion of Mi:.-ilo-u—c-H7roN, the Board decided to give an annusl subscription of to the Padgate Nursing Association, subject to the apploval of the Local Government Board.

ACCOMMODA'fION.

in his report to viii-e-H—n-it-s-e—C'ettt-mittee Dr. Braide called attention to the inadequacy of the accommoda- tion for the sick. Ho also reported an outbreak of whooping cough among the children in the House and the Committee authorized the temporary appointment of a female assistant to look after the cases.'

The minutes were approved. I NCREA SING EXPENDITURE.

The CLERK reported that the total relief cheques for the fortnight amounted to /190 and that the schools accounts for the quarter came to £617 13s. 10d. This, with the addition of the Workhouse accounts for the quarter, made a total of £2,251 lSs. Rd. He remarked that the expenditure under these heads was continually increasing. although no doubt quite justifiably. Still, he remembered the time when £BOO or .2900 would have covered the quarter's expenditure for indoor relief._

, Mr. Dams said the amount of money expended, as compared with nine or ten years ago, was enormous. It was time the Board cried " halt." The public purse was very elastic but the`public were watching closely and they would be" down "on the Board if they kept on expending in that way. The CLERK said the expenditure was unavoidable and they bad to look forward to increases of rateable value to cover local expenditure. Mr. THOMPSON emphasised the importance of placing good men on the Assessment Committee.

Coroner Brighouse and infant Mortality. A verdict of " accidental death " was returned at an inquest held by Mr. Coroner Brighouse, . at the • Police Station, Warrington, oh Tuesday, respecting the death of the three-days-old child of Mary Edith Brobbin,l4, Raddon cottages, Latchford. It appeared that the child slept with the mother and another woman, • and on the third day it was in bed with the mother and was found dead by the • midwife. Dr. Leadbetter certified that death was due to suffocation. The Coroner said be was not surprised that the child was found dead. It was absolutely ridiculous for a child to sleep with two adults. If women wanted to get rid of children let them have them in bed inst<pad of in. a cot, and feed them with the bottle instead of at the breast.

The Examiner, Saturday, January 13, 1906.
