CROSFIELD WINNING I

Mr. Crosfield is winning all along the line and the utmost enthusiasm and confidence prevail among Liberals with regard to Monday's poll. A return of the Liberal candidate in Warrington for the first time in 25 years is eagerly anticipated. All Progressives intend- , ing to vote for Mr. Crosfield should vote early on Monday morning. A vote given before mid-day is of more value than one given afterwards. WORK HARD, POLL EARLY, AND VICTORY

No Disfranchisement for Poverty. "I am in favour of exemption from disfranchisement of recipients of tem- porary poor relief, and do not see why a man who had always kept his head straight, and who, temporarily falling into distress through want of employ- ment, had to accept temporary relief, should lose his citizenship," Taxation of Land Values. "There is practically but one great impediment in the way of a sweeping improvement which would elevate the physical and moral welfare of the people This is the interest, and the overdue regard to the interest, of the landowner, and the political and social influence that he and his class can exercise. Let the value of land be assessed independently of the buildings upon it, and upon such )valuation let contributions be made to those public services which create the value."— Leeds, igth March, 1903. Readjust the Rates. " What is our rating system ? It is a tax upon industry and labour, upon enterprise, upon improvement; it is a tax which is the direct cause of much suffering and overcrowding in the towns. And remember that overcrowd- ing is not a symptom only, but a cause of poverty, because it demoralises its victims and forces them to find relief in excesses. By throwing the rates on site values, communities which have created these values will be set free—free in the sense that they can expand, free to direct their own destinies."—Newport, 30th Novem- ber, 1903. Emancipate the Rural Labourer. "There are three main divisions of operation for the amelioration of the condition of the rural popu:ation. First of all it is necessary to provide healthy, comfortable homes in the country. Secondly, there is the furnishing to the 'labourer in the country the opportunity of a career, so that by industry and intelligence he may raise himself, for, after all, they must not forget the influ- of schools since 1870. Thirdly, there should be freedom in that career. Greater power is wanted for local authorities to acquire and lease ground for a cottage, and for an adequate 'garden to accompany it."—Westminster I Palace Hotel, 11th May, tgat,

Temperance. "What we have got now to do is to repair as far as it is possible the damage that the Licensing Act has done. I think there are two great objects in that; the first is to restore the local licensing authority to the full powers and dis- cretion that they possessed. and to extend those powers considerably. Then the next thing is to impose a limit of time to the artificial provisions of the Act. Those are two things of themselves which will be of great difficulty to carry through any House of Commons —and the House of Lords perhaps still more.

"The great, the best, and the supreme judges are the inhabitants of a district whose daily lives are affected by the liquor traffic. That is the cardinal principle we must bear in mind."- 4th April, 1905.

Free Trade. "Liberals in this matter are not fighting a single proposal, but a whole spirit and tone of policy and adminis- tration and legislation. These fiscal proposals are saturated, as the whole policy of the present Government has been found to be, with restriction as against freedom, with inequality between trade and trade with in- justice towards the community and con- sumers, with privilege and monopoly, with jealousy and unfriendliness to- wards other nations. They are essentially part of a retrograde and anti-democratic system, and they will never, I trust, be accepted by an in- telligent and free nation."—Manchester, 29th November, India.

"We shall make ourselves party to no steps that involve any invasion of the sacred principle—for it is a principle recognised by each party throughout the realm of the King—the sacred principle of the subordination of the military to the civil authority."—Albert Hall, 22nd December, 1905.

Proper Treatment for Volunteers. " We have in the Militia and the Volunteers two forces which, furnishing a congenial field for the different sec- tions and classes of the population, are, I believe, capable of being developed and extended into very formidable forces. They should not be nibbled at and altered in their character, but, they should be encouraged, their duties enlarged, and their efficiency raised."— Edinburgh, sth November, 1904.

No Extravagance. "I at least am patient enough not to desire to see the weakening of my country by such waste of money as we have had for the last ten years. What has it brought us? Shall I recite some of the links in the dismal, ugly chain? Dear money, lowered credit, reduced home demands, therefore reduced out- put, further reduction in wages, and increase in pauperism and unemploy- ment. (Hear, hear.) The fact is, you cannot pile up ,debt and taxation, as they have been piled up, without feeling the strain in every fibre of society."

===== 0002601_19060113_art0182.txt | included=true | included =====
IS SURE.

This election is the greatest fight of the present century. For 25 years Warrington has suffered from Tory Rule. Mr. Pierpoint has steadily and persistently voted against every pro- gressive proposal. DEAR, FOOD, CHINESE LABOUR, AND OPPOSI- TION TO WORKING-PEOPLE'S RIGHTS, seems to have been his motto. The time has at length arrived when the electors of Warrington can express their opinion' of his conduct. Surely a change of representation will be a good thing? The Liberal candi- date is associated with our local indus- tries; he understands the people's wants and requirements; the family he is associated with has done a splendid work for our town; he is a gentleman of integrity and practical commonsense —in politics an advanced Progressive, and in social matters a keen sportsman and a real reformer. With such a man and such a cause, we cannot for a moment doubt what the result will be. Workingmen, we ask you to vote for cheap food, less taxes, extended industry, and better days for all! You can ensure these boons as far as is possible to you by PUTTING MR. CROSFIELD AT THE HEAD OF THE POLL. Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman has built up the grandest government of modem times. His Cabinet consists of men of great ability, discernment and political knowledge. Inside the Cabinet for the first time, labour has received recognition in the person of Mr. Burns. The Government is waiting for the ver- dict from the polls to give them the opportunity of setting to work. The measures of reform we have waited for so 10w..° we are about to see taken in hand. The whole country seems to be about to respond to the appeal of the Liberal leaders.

LET NOT WARRINGTON LAG

BEHIND!

Too kin.-'' we have been misrepre- sented. Atown like this with a large labouring and industrial population ought to be in the forefront in matters of progression. On Monday next you will give your verdict ; let it be an emphatic condemnation of the Tory tactics of the last to years, and the retrogressive performances of our mem- ber. The supporter of dear food and Chinese labour ought no longer to represent the town of Warrington. As we are one of the earliest towns to poll, we have now a chance of leading the way. _ _ _

ELECTORS DO YOUR DUTY AND THIS GOOD OLD TOWN OF OURS WILL TAKE ITS PLACE IN THE FOREFRONT OF THE GREAT ARMY OF REFORM! ! !

===== 0002601_19060113_art0183.txt | included=true | included =====
FOR THE FIGHT.

WHAT IS THE LIBERAL POLICY. The Prime Minister's Programme. What are the policies before the country? The Tory policy we know. It is Protection, disguised as Tariff Reform or Retaliation. The Liberal Policy has been stated by the Prime Minister in a series of speeches in the country. Here is his declaration on the leading issues.

Stop Chinese Labour. One conclusion his Majesty's Govern- ment has arrived at, and it is this: to stop forthwith, so far as it is practicable to do it forthwith, the recruitment and embarkation of coolies in China, and their importation into South Africa. And instructions have been, given to that effect.—Albert Hall, 22nd Dec., 194

No Sectarian Tests. The condemnation of the Education Act, as ignoring popular rights, as excluding from their proper share of influence the parent and the ratepayer, the two classes most concerned, and as writing upon the door of entry to a great and honourable, and beneficent profession a sectarian test—that is a standing condemnation which time can never wither. —Scarborough. 15th May, 1903. Liberty for Trade Unions. "One other matter in this connection I would mention—it is, in our opinion, a pressing matter, that is the law of combination (loud and continued cheering)—the law of combination which has been gravely affected by a series of judicial decisions, and it will , be our desire, with the least possible ' delay, so to amend it as to give freedom and security to trade unions in the pursuit of their legitimate aims." (Renewed cheers). —Albert Hall, 22nd Dec., 1905.

===== 0002601_19060113_art0184.txt | included=true | included =====
During the North Dorset election Sir Randolf Baker, the Conservative can- didate, offered to pay the expenses of any one of his constituents who would go out if he could stand six months' work with Kaffirs in a mine selected by Sir Randolf. A farm labourer named H. T. Sedlen accepted the offer, went to South Africa, and completed 6 months mine-labour alongside the Kaffirs. He has come back convinced that white men could do the work equally as well, and in fact, while in the mine, he offered to stay on, but the management would not have him. Sedlen managed to save -20 in 6 months out of his miner's wage.

===== 0002601_19060113_art0185.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT OUR LEADERS SAY.

"FREE TRADE, LIKE HONESTY, REMAINS

THE BEST POLICY."

The Warrington Junior Liberal Association has issued a circular calling attention to the following concise opinions on the Free Trade Question by local and national Liberal leaders _

A. H. CROSFIELD, ESQ. :—An in- dividual does not grow rich by in- creasing his or her cost of living and expenditure. Neither does a nation. Protection notoriously spreads corrup- tion amongst the governing classes and civil servants, and prevents progress by causing lethargy and lack of enterprise amongst employers, as well as envy and disunion amongst the working classes themselves. Why, then, should we be so foolish as to put a premium on political and commercial corruption and re-action just to make the nation as a whole poorer? The onus of proof lies with those who make this prepos- terous proposal: But they give us no proof because they cannot. C. E. PARKER, Eso :—Protection, is repugnant to justice, because it sets up artificial and unjust inequalities, and enables one class to invade the rights of others. It paralyzes political purity, because it turns the power of law- making into a means of enriching some at the expense of others. It creates and encourages monopoly. Commercially it is unwise, economically it is unsound and wasteful, and ethically it is unjust.

WM. LONG, ESQ., J.P. : I can imagine nothing more disastrous or suicidal to the welfare of the great majority of the people than to allow our glorious Free Trade policy to be tampered with—the effect of which would be to open the door to corruption, both morally and financially. Pro- tection under any disguise might for a , time have the effect of making the rich more wealthy, but I am convinced that the conditions of life would be very much worse for the millions of our I people.

I ALDERMAN W. PIERPOINT ESQ., J.P. :—Can it be a good thing to make the food of the people dearer? Where is the benefit to come from? Are the children to be limited in the necessary food to satisfy the greed of some people who believe in Protection? I do not think for a moment that it is justified in the eyes of any man that Protection should be brought about.

HENRY ROBERTS ESQ., J.P. : The people of this generation whose lot it is to labour and to earn their daily bread by the sweat of the brow have inherited the priceless blessing of untaxed bread. This inestimable boon was won for us in spite of obloquy, hatred, and falsehood by the untiring Patriotism of men who placed the people's well-being above party in- terests. Let us see to it that we prove ourselves worthy of this great inherit- ance, and that we hand it down to our children unimpaired.

WILLIAM MADDEN ESQ., (BAR- RISTER-AT-LAW) :—The supremacy of any nation now in the international commerce of the world generally or in part can, only be .won and held by scientific knowledge and intelligent industry. Against such intellectual forces Protection is the weapon of the Cowardly and ignorant. It is an anti- quated tool which has lost shape and edge.

IA Wrexh ---- lam miner named John E. J. GUEST, ESQ., J.P. :—No country Williams, who has had experience in can fully develop its resources with a ' South African mines, has been giving policy of Protection. With great 1 his views on the Chinese labour ques- ; natural advantages they may prosper I tion. EDMUND OSBORNE ESQ., (PRE- SIDENT OF THE JUNIOR LIBERAL Asso- CIATION) :-It behoves every young man to take an active and intelligent interest in local and national affairs, to study public questions in the light of equal rights and opportunities, and to fight against any scheme which involves the sacrifice of honesty and freedom. What- ever we do, let us not become Cham- berlain chameleons. Protection has bolstered up trusts and monopolies, which have been the means of robbing workers of the just fruits of their labour, and of making a handful of individuals enormously wealthy. We must not be " rushed " into a change of the fiscal system. Let us weigh well the facts,. for and against, for it is a matter which affects not only ourselves but the future welfare of the nation. •

SIR HENRY CAMPBELL-BAN- NERMAN (PREMIER) :—Free Trade gives the freeest play to individual initiative, individual energy, and in- dividual character. Protection is a system of out-relief based upon favour- itism and involving a transformation of healthy trades giving strength to the community, into parasitic industries sapping its vitality. Immediately a tariff is put on it becomes incrusted with vested interests.

THE RIGHT HON. D. LLOYD GEORGE, M.P. :—A little island—one' thirtieth the size of America, one half of .the population of the United States. It is not to be compared in magnitude to Germany or any of those great countries, and yet its ships cover the ocean, and fill every port. They build barriers of tariff and tolls against them. They ride over them all on the crest of the wave and the end of it is—simply because you have got freedom at the prow—Britain wins; and as long as it will stand for this great principle it will win for itself everlasting renown in the history of the nations.

SIR EDWARD GREY :—I Free Trade to have been a desirab practice for this country to adopt the days of Cobden. It has becor essential since, because it is the ba of our prosperity.

THE RIGHT HON. SIR WILFRI LAURIER, (CANAWAN PREMIER) : - Protection is the greatest of all mi takes. If the British Empire is to I maintained, it can only be upon ti most absolute freedom, political an commercial.

LORD H. CECIL :—A protection tariff is a mechanism for turning wealth out of its natural channel. Let us beware of our taking the hated yoke of Protection upon our necks again. Let us adhere to one system of commercial liberty for the sake of both commerce and politics.

JOHN BRIGHT :—I would a.ssrtl without fear of error that our adher4. ence to our Free Trade policy is the more necessary and advantageous to us: seeing that other countries have not yet followed our example. Surely if other' nations injure us by their high duties it would be no compensation to us to put difficulties in the way of our buying what we want.

THE LATE SIR ROBERT PEEL : I maintain that the best way to com- pete with hostile tariffs is to encourage- free imports.

MARRIED WOMAll's; RASH ACT.—At the Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday, Mary Ann Rustage, of Orchard-street, Stockton Heath was summoned for attempting to committee suicide by taking phosphorus on December 3rd. Elizabeth Ann Rostock, wife of James Rostock, 411, Heath-lane, Stockton Heath, said that on December 2nd,dereudant came to her house and witness elbowed her to stay for the night. Defendant was unwell the next morning, and did not get op.. De- fendant's little girl cams to the house end went upstairs. She called to witness and said that her reother was going to put some matches in the water and drink it. Witness asked defendant whit she had done, and received the reply" Nothing," Dr. Howard was sent for, and used a stomlch pump. The defendant was dis- charged on promising not to attempt to take away her life again. SUNDAY SCFIOOL PRIZE DISTRIttrTION.—The annual prize distribution in connection with the Independent Methodist Sunday school took placo in the new chapel on Sunday afternoon. The chair was occupied by Mr. I James Mort, of Lyrnm. The Programme consisted of songs, solos and recitations. The opening hymn " We'll work till Jesus comes," was given in a very creditable 'manner by Miss Woods and Miss Mounfield's classes. , Miss Ada Grounds sang "Show me Thy grace." and Mr. ; jams Ronghsedge "Saved by grtme." Miss Mounfleld's j class recited Psalm cxv., Miss Strange's class Psalm xxx.. MiS4 Houghton's class Psalm xiii., and Mr. Cole's Iclass Psalm xxvi. Recitations were also given by Edith Cole, Albert Isherwood, and Tom Pearce. Mr. I William Roberts distributed the prizes to 97 scholars ' for regular attendance and good I ehavionr, several j having received ths highest possible number of marks attainable.

WEAK, DIZZY, NO APPETITE. A'HASTINGS WOMAN CURED OF "PAINS LIKE KNIFE STABS," BY MOTHER SEIGEL'S SYRUP, There is no healthier spot in England than Hastings. But there are some ailments upon which climate has 'little or no effect, prominent among them being indi- gestion and biliousnees : and so it happens that Mrs. 'Emily Boorman, of 5, Emybrooke Terrace, Hastings, 'writes thus OD January 24th, 1905 : "It is about seven Nears since my health broke down. I found myself in low woirits, without appetite, and lacking interest in .everything. Gradually this feeling increased ; my , digestion broke down, so that I became afraid of food of whatever kind. There was a constant sensation of pressure at the the chest, with sharp pains like stabs of a knife, darting through my back. Sometimes these rains came on while I was in the street, so acutely that I enuld not refrain from crying out. "Wind in my stomach and howls was another of my troubles : it was constant, and I was rarely free from headache. Heart palpitation. if not so frequent, was 'more alarniing, nnd so interfered with my sleep that for two years or more I did not know what a proper night's rest was. Each morning found me dizzy and unrefreshed. " Giving up solid food for a time. I lived upon : but this caused more wind and pressure than ever. I became thin and weak from want of food, and the dizziness increased so that when in the street I had to stou every now and then and press my hand over my eyes to steady myself. " "k ft er a doctor had treated me for two years without the least benefit, I turned to Mother SPigel's Syrup, and began a course of that medicine. There was an imnrovement at once in my condition. It seemed to remove the load from my chest. That improvement continuM until soon I had neither pain nor palpitation, my digestion was Pound. end T was in all respects as well as ever I had been in my life. DANCED WITH THE KIND. A telegram from Atlanta (Georgia) states that Mrs. Roaring Peyton died there in deep poverty. Mrs. Peyton was formerly a great beauty, and came of wealthy and distinguished parents. She had the honour in 1850 of •dancing with King Edward, then Prince of Wales, at It ball given in Washington. _ 4e, 11. MJ P Uce, 4:1747 c:o • • • • 4 s C-INN R.,.

IRISH CATHOLIC SUPPORT FOR MR. CROSFIELD. A Sunday Afternoon Meeting, On Sunday morning, in the different Catholic Churches in Warrington answers by Mr. Pierpoint and Mr. A. IT. Crosaeld to the questions submitted to them on the Cathoilc question were read. It was decided to make no comment on the answers but to leave it to the Catholic electors to decide between the two candidates. On Sunday afternoon a crowded meeting was held in- the Co-operative Hall, Winvvicic-street,mainly composed lof Irish Catholics, under the presidency of Mr. Sexton, secretary of the Warrington Branch of the Irish National League. The manifesto from the National League was read. It was pro posed, by Councillor Maginn and seconded by a member of the Warrington Branch of the League: "That this meeting having heard the declaration of our leaders, hereby declares its determination to snpport Mr. A. H. Crosfleld, and resolves to do all in its power to rettun him at the head of the poll." The resolution was unanimously and enthusiastically carried. An address by Mr. J. Howes on Irish Questions brought the meeting to an end. The Story of English Pottery." This was the title of an interesting lecture which was delivered in the Masonic Hall on Monday evening, under the auspices of the Literary and Philosophical Society, by Mr. H.' Stuart Page. The chair was occu- pied by Alderman Bennett, who presided over a good attendance. Mr. Page dealt with his • subject in a most entertaining manner reviewing the growth of the potter's art from the time when man first learned to make vessels of sun-dried clay. The address was illustrated by some excellent specimens of pottery and porcelain. On the motion of Mr. C. Madeley, seconded by Mr. G. Carruthers. and supported by Alderman Bennett, a hearty vote of than -I,:s was passed to Mr. Page for his lecture.

_an them grew lanky bamboo grass. Here had a meal to prepare us for our midnight vork. We had also to make preparations in our dress.. The 'porters had to go forward laden with rugs and all kinds of wraps', for we were dressed for the hot Japan summer, and soon we should be in intense cold. We had, too, to put on Japanese shoes over our boots. They arc made of twisted straw, with thick soles, and were absolutely. necessary to keep the pumice- stone from cutting our boots to pieces. I wore out two pairs in my climb. There was very little vegetation after we left the fir wood, nothing but short thistle-like plants, and as We climbed the air became more and more keen. It was indeed bitter after the heart of the lowlands. On, on we scrambled over the hardened cinder-like pumice, sinking deep in it at every step, so that sometimes we had to stop to empty our boots. At last we reached a narrow pathway leading round the side of the mountain. It was very narrow and difficult to distinguish. There Was Just room for my two feet, and below me the mountain went shelving away hundreds of 'feet down to a desolate region of lava rocks that we could not see then, but looked at with dread on oar return homeward. A false step would have sent us rolling helplessly down the 'mountain side, , Our guides seemed to breathe more freely when once we had passed this dangerous curve. Now we had reached the old crater, and a se- cond halt was called.' Our porters were there with the wraps, and we were indeed glad to see them, for we were getting near the top, eight thousand feet above sea level, and we were all shivering with cold in the chill night air. We rolled ourselves in our rugs and lay down as close together as possible in order to get a little sleep, for it was about two o'clock in the morn- ing, and we were all very tired after our adven- tures. We could not; however, rest for long, for we still had some hundreds of feet to climb to reach the edge of the crater by sunrise. The rest of the journey was safe but very tiring, for we had simply to struggle upward through loose, slipping pumice stone. Just as we started we iound ourselves suddenly wrapped in .a cloud of sulphurous smoke. Oh I how horribly it smelt ! How we longed to run anywhere to get out of it! We panted amid puffed. but that was no good, and we were very glad when the air suddenly cleared. This was the first thing that made me realise that I was Climbing areal Working volcano and that,! was near the crater itself. At last we were at the top. We could see the precipices on the inner side going deep down, to be lost in the seething sulphurous smoke below. We were warned not to go too near the edge. The ground might have given way undor us. But some of the party crawled on hands and knees to the very edge and looked over into the great cauldron. They had hardly returned from their hazardous position when another blinding cloud of smoke burst forth, and stones and hot • cinders were heard scattered. around us. By this time dawn was beginning. - A faint light showed us the heaving blue mists which covered the world beneath us. Away on either side there were lines of darker blue mountains, which seemed to rest on nothing more solid than luminous clouds. As we watched, mists, clouds and mountains be- came tinged with rosy red, . and then the, sun itself rolled up, and the mists began to thin and gradually roll away. It was tone for us to be; going We could not travel in the heat of the day, and there was no time to spare. The des- cent was uneventful, except we could See more plainly the desolation .ot the country through which we had travelled. But who ,can tell the trials of the last part of the journey I Going slows hill on a Japanese pack horse is no pleasant ex- perience. Iliad only one hand to hold on with for d had to have an umbrella to prevent sun- stroke. The steepness threw me forward on the korse's neck, bent my legs under me, ana ial'obed them at every step against the edge of the saddle. I tried to ask the betto whether I was sale. which I succeeded in doing by means of many signs. He seemed quite satisfied, and was aba solutely hard hearted about may sufferings. He and the sun had no pity; and so it went on for hours, till I fek as it I could cry with misery. It was over at last, and the friends we left at home came out to greet us, looking so cool and clean and .comfortahle, and to laugh at us with our stiffened limbs, weary faces, and clothes al! discoloured with the smoke' cf _our volcanic friend, Asami 'Varna. [This story is written by E. C. Matravo sal 0 THE STORY. OF A MARE'S FAITHFULNESS. • Bess lived on the coast of Hampshire. She was a beloved companian of her mistress. Bess had a tbal which she xaas very fond of. But a great trouble was to befsil both the mare and the foal. The lady's father lost most of his pro- perty and they had to go to a'smaller home. He said to his daughter, "We most give lin Bess as there is a gentleman in the Ise of Wight who has offeredax large sum for her and you know that money. Is very necessary to me just now. We shall 'keep the ' When Bess was on the steamer she wanted her foal. She would not settle down in her new home. After a day or two Bess was nowhere to be found, the stables and pasture land searched but no Bess was to be found. • His daughter Winnie was seated in the garden, she was thinking of the poor mare and the motherless foal when all at once she heard a neigh she ran out and listened it was the voice of Be, In a moment she was at the gate laughing with excitement. Bess had swam the Solent-. They telegraphed at once to the Isle of Wight to say the mare had come back again. But the man said because of the mare's faithfulness he should have the money and the mare. laEan UNCLE NED. I should like to be a member of the Children's Corner. lam sending you this tale. With all good wishes from. ANNIE ROWLEY. k

Here is a sound comprehensive policy which all classes may accept, since it will benefit all classes alike. Do you want Mr. Balfour Back Again? THE PRICE OF TORYISM. Toryism is sometimes fashionable, but can you afford the luxury? Look at these figures. 1895 was the last com- plete year of Liberal Administration, and 1905 is the last year of Tory Administration: Revenue. 1895. 1905. increase. Out of taxes £78,855,000 £120,530,000 £41,875,000 Out of Post Oblice Telegraphs, &c. 16,029,000 L'22,810,000 £6,811,000 Total £94.684000 £143,370,000 £48,686,000 In taxes, therefore, we pay 42 millions a year more under Tory than Liberal rule. We pay one sovereign extra taxes per year under Tory rule for every man, woman, and child in the United King- dom, including the unemployed, the aged, the paupers, and the imbecile. For a house of 5 persons, this means £5 a year, or a fortnight's pay for a clerk or skilled workman; three weeks' pay for an unskilled artizan.

How is the money raised? io millions comes from the income-tax which has been increased from Bd. to is. in the E. Why, then, does the professional man vote Tory? He is robbing himself. Other extra taxes are as follows: Increased or Yield from New Tax. Increase. Tea Tobacco 4d. to Gd. a lb. ... £2,000,000 Various ... £500,000 I Alcohol Various ... £3,000,000 I Coal Stamps £500,000 Sugar id. per lb. ... £6,000,000 On tea and sugar alone, we pay eight millions extra taxation, which means 4s. per head fbr every man, woman, and child. Think what this 4s. would have meant for every member of every family this Christmas, as well as for the trades- men and the manufacturers. If money is scarce, why do you vote Tory? Despite these extra taxes, the Tories are running us into debt : Year. National Debt. 1895 659 millions 190 S —. 79G millions Increase ... 137 millions or £3 ss, extra debt per head of popu- lation. The losS to investors has been ,

enormous. In 1898, Consols stood at filo. They have fallen to L'9o. The rate of interest has in the meantime been reduced from 2,1 to 21 per cent.; which accounts for £m of the fall. But there remains L-io loss which is solely due to Tory mismanagement, and this means 2s. in the f on the investment. In 1898 the National Debt was 638 millions. The fall has involved on this debt alone A LOSS OF 63 MILLIONS. THAT IS TORYISM ? CAN YOU AFFORD IT? , ENGLAND FOR THE ENGLISH. ----- The Land for the People. WHAT THE LAND SYSTEM HAS DONE. In sixty years it has reduced the popu- lation of Ireland from 8 millions to 4 millions. In a hundred years it has turned 2,000,000 acres of Scottish land into deer, forests for the amusement of million- aires. In England it has desolated the country side. The land offers no career

to the peasantry. A labourer cannot find a cottage to live in or a patch of ground to cultivate. He cannot become a tenant farmer and a tenant farmer cannot become a landed proprietor. The people therefore crowd into the cities, where also the landlord awaits them. Their earnings are swallowed up in rents, and ground values mount up by leaps and bounds. Hence arise poverty, unemployment, and physical degeperation. WHAT IS THE REMEDY? We must win back the land for the people, both in the country and in the cities. HOW CAN THIS BE EFFECTED? THE COUNTRY. In the country, every farmer should be given the right to purchase his hold- ing with the assistance of the State. This plan is pursued in Ireland, and why not in England? Every local authority should be en- abled to secure land by compulsory purchase for the establishment of small holdings, which may be co-ordinated by means of light railways and associa- tions of peasant proprietors.

There should be rent courts to secure for the tenant farmer compensation for improvements effected by him in his holding, as well as immunity against persecution on account of his religious and political opinions. THE CITIES. In the cities and towns,all site values should be taxed. This would force un- occupied land into the market, lower rents, and relieve overcrowding. Moreover, it would secure for the nation part, at any rate, of the land's unearned increment in value created by the people themselves, but now passing into the pocket of the landlord. This reform can be effected through the medium of the Budget, over which the House of Lords has no control. Every landlord should be compelled to register his name and property at stated periods. The Municipalities themselves should be permitted and encouraged to acquire land, as in Germany. THE TIVE ANSWER TO THE CRY OF PROTECTION IS THE LAND FOR THE PEOPLE!
